We use the loss function to numerically score the coupling and correspondence, then infimize over all choices.

Indeed, if we think of a loss function as a notion of distance, then the definition of
d_{\mathrm{R}}
most closely resembles that of the Gromov-Wasserstein distance. The only extra component is a supremum over the predictor correspondence. To make the comparison more explicit, suppose
(X,d_{X},\mu_{X})
and
(X^{\prime},d_{X^{\prime}},\mu_{X^{\prime}})
are metric measure spaces. We can construct the following problems:

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle:=(X,X,\mu_{X}\otimes\mu_{X},d_{X},\{\id_{X}\})

\displaystyle P^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=(X^{\prime},X^{\prime},\mu_{X^{\prime}}\otimes\mu_{X^{\prime}},d_{X^{\prime}},\{\id_{X^{\prime}}\}).

We then write

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu_{X}\otimes\mu_{X},\mu_{X^{\prime}}\otimes\mu_{X^{\prime}})}\int\big|d_{X}(x,y)-d_{X^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big|\,\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime}).

Here
\otimes
represents the product of measures.
This is exactly twice the Gromov-Wasserstein distance (Definition
8
) except for one difference: the measure
\gamma
. The infimum runs over all of

\Pi(\mu_{X}\otimes\mu_{X},\mu_{X^{\prime}}\otimes\mu_{X^{\prime}})
, while in the true Gromov-Wasserstein distance,
\gamma
must take the specific form
\gamma=\sigma\otimes\sigma
for some
\sigma\in\Pi(\mu_{X},\mu_{X^{\prime}})
. Indeed, the expression above (divided by 2) is identified as the “second lower bound” of the Gromov-Wasserstein distance by
Mémoli (2007)
.

Another connection between the Gromov-Wasserstein and Risk distances can be drawn as well. Specifically, one can encode compact metric measure spaces
X
as supervised learning problems
i(X)
in a natural way which preserves the identifications induced by the two pseudometrics.

Proposition 33
.

Given a compact metric measure space
(X,d_{X},\mu_{X})
, define a problem

i(X):=(X,X,(\Delta_{X})_{\sharp}\mu_{X},d_{X},H_{X}),

where

•

\Delta_{X}:X\to X\times X
is the diagonal map, and

•

H_{X}
is the set of all constant functions
X\to X
.

If
X
and
X^{\prime}
are metric measure spaces, then

d_{\mathrm{GW},1}(X,X^{\prime})=0\iff d_{\mathrm{R}}(i(X),i(X^{\prime}))=0.

The proof can be found in Appendix
A.1.1
where we also discuss a connection with a variant of the Gromov-Wasserstein distance by
Hang et al. (2019, Section 4.1)
that is recovered as
d_{\mathrm{R}}(i(X),i(Y))
.
It is a folklore result in optimal transport that deciding whether
d_{\mathrm{GW},1}(X,X^{\prime})=0
for finite metric measure spaces
X
and
X^{\prime}
is at least as hard as the graph isomorphism problem.
This is not difficult to prove; let
G=(V,E)
and
G^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
be finite simple graphs. We can encode
G
as a metric measure space
V=(V,d_{V},\mu_{V})
, where
d_{V}
is the shortest path metric and
\mu_{V}
is the uniform probability measure. Similarly construct
V^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},d_{V^{\prime}},\mu_{V^{\prime}})
. Then
d_{\mathrm{GW},1}(V,V^{\prime})=0
if and only if
G
and
G^{\prime}
are isomorphic. Indeed, if
G
and
G^{\prime}
are isomorphic then the isomorphism induces the requisite coupling between
\mu_{V}
and
\mu_{V^{\prime}}
. Conversely, if
d_{\mathrm{GW},1}(V,V^{\prime})=0
, then

V
and
V^{\prime}
are isomorphic as metric measure spaces, and in particular are isometric as metric spaces. An isometry between
V
and
V^{\prime}
that preserves the shortest path metric is in fact a graph isomorphism.

Hence Proposition
33
yields the following corollary.

Corollary 34
.

The problem of deciding whether
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})=0
for two problems with finite input and response spaces is at least as hard as graph isomorphism.

It is desirable to draw a stronger connection between the Risk distance and Gromov-Wasserstein distance by finding a function
i
such that
d_{\mathrm{GW},1}(X,X^{\prime})=d_{\mathrm{R}}(i(X),i(X^{\prime}))
. We will nearly accomplish this goal in Section
6.2
by linking the
L^{p}
-Risk distance with a slightly modified Gromov-Wasserstein distance which is known in the literature.

4
Stability

One major use of the Risk distance is to facilitate stability results.
By “stability result,” we mean a result of one of the two following forms. Suppose we modify a problem
P
via some process to produce another problem
f(P)
. We say that
d_{\mathrm{R}}
is stable under
f
if every problem
P
satisfies an inequality of the form

d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,f(P))\leq J(f)

for some fixed functional
J
which measures how dissimilar
f
is from the identity function
\mathrm{id}:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}
and satisfies
J(\mathrm{id})=0
. We would say that “the Risk distance is stable under
f
”.
Stability results of this form assure us that small modifications to a problem do not move that problem far under
d_{\mathrm{R}}
. For instance, Condition 2 from Section
3.3
is a stability result. In particular, Condition 2 implies that
d_{\mathrm{R}}
is stable under changes to the loss function.

Alternatively, a “stability result” could mean the following. Suppose that, from any problem
P
, we can compute a descriptor
g(P)
which lies in a metric space
(Z,d_{Z})
. We say that
g
is
stable under
d_{\mathrm{R}}
if there is some non-negative constant
C
satisfying

d_{Z}(g(P),g(P^{\prime}))\leq Cd_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})

for all problems
P
and
P^{\prime}
. Stability results of this form act as quantitative guarantees that problems “close” under
d_{\mathrm{R}}
have similar descriptors.

4.1
Stability of the Constrained Bayes Risk and Loss Profile

From a machine learning perspective, the most important feature of a predictor is the loss that it incurs. This information can be captured by a predictor’s
loss profile
, a probability measure on the real line describing the loss that a predictor incurs on a random observation. If
P
is a problem and
h\in H
is a predictor, the
loss profile of
h
is the pushforward
(\ell_{h})_{\sharp}\eta\in\prob({\mathbb{R}})
.

Note that the loss profile of
h
is the distribution of the random variable
\ell(h(\mathsf{X}),\mathsf{Y})
, where
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{Y}
are distributed according to
\eta
. This is closely related to the random variable
\ell(h(\mathsf{X}),\mathsf{Y})-{\mathcal{B}}(P)
, called the
excess loss random variable
in the literature, differing only by an additive constant. The distribution of the excess loss is related to convergence rates of empirical risk minimization
(
Van Erven et al., 2015
;
Grünwald and Mehta, 2020
)
.

Letting
h
range over all of
H
, we get an informative description of a problem.

Definition 35
.

Given a problem
P
, the
loss profile set of
P
is the set

L(P):=\left\{(\ell_{h})_{\sharp}\eta\middle|h\in H\right\}.

Note that the loss profile
L(P)
of
P
contains enough information to recover the constrained Bayes risk. This follows immediately from the definition (we omit the proof).

Proposition 36
.

Given a problem
P
, one has

{\mathcal{B}}(P)=\inf_{\alpha\in L(P)}\mathrm{mean}(\alpha).

Since probability measures on the real line, such as loss profiles, can be compared using the Wasserstein metric
d_{\mathrm{W},1}
,
collections
of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
form subsets of the metric space
(\prob(\mathbb{R}),d_{\mathrm{W},1})
, and therefore can be compared using the Hausdorff distance
d_{\mathrm{H}}
with underlying metric
d_{\mathrm{W},1}
. That is, if
A,B\subseteq\prob(\mathbb{R})
, the Hausdorff distance between
A
and
B
is given by

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{H}}^{d_{\mathrm{W},1}}(A,B)=\inf_{R\in{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)}\sup_{(\mu,\nu)\in R}d_{\mathrm{W},1}(\mu,\nu).

Now that we have a way to compare loss profile sets, we can prove their stability under
d_{\mathrm{R}}
. The theorem below establishes this stability property, and in addition, also proves that the constrained Bayes risk is stable in the sense of the Risk distance.

Theorem 37

(Stability of the Constrained Bayes Risk and Loss Profile)
.

If
P
and
P^{\prime}
are problems, then

|{\mathcal{B}}(P)-{\mathcal{B}}(P^{\prime})|\leq d_{\mathrm{H}}^{d_{\mathrm{W},1}}(L(P),L(P^{\prime}))\leq d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})

The proof, which is relegated to Appendix
A
, proceeds in two steps. We establish the first inequality using the fomulation of the constrained Bayes risk presented in Proposition
36
. To prove the second inequality, we notice that the quantity in the middle defines a pseudometric on the collection of all problems, then apply the maximality claim in Theorem
28
to show that the Risk distance is larger.
By ignoring the middle quantity in Theorem
37
, we obtain stability the constrained Bayes risk.

Example 38

(Constrained Bayes Risk and Bounded Loss)
.

Here we remark that if the problems
P
and
P^{\prime}
have bounded loss functions, then the stability of the constrained Bayes risk, namely that
if
P
and
P^{\prime}
are problems, then

|{\mathcal{B}}(P)-{\mathcal{B}}(P^{\prime})|\leq d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime}),

can be easily and directly obtained through arguments involving the metric properties of the Risk distance. Indeed, if
c>\max(\sup\ell,\sup\ell^{\prime})
, then, by Example
31
and the triangle inequality for the Risk distance, we have that

c-{\mathcal{B}}(P)=d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P_{\bullet}(c))\leq d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})+d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime},P_{\bullet}(c))=d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})+c-{\mathcal{B}}(P^{\prime})

so that
{\mathcal{B}}(P^{\prime})-{\mathcal{B}}(P)\leq d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime}).
By swapping the roles of
P
and
P^{\prime}
we obtain the inequality
{\mathcal{B}}(P)-{\mathcal{B}}(P^{\prime})\leq d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime},P)
which then, together with the fact that
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime},P)=d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})
, implies the stability claim. Note that Theorem
37
implies the same claim without assuming the boundedness condition on the loss functions.

4.2
Stability of Rademacher Complexity

In statistical learning theory, the
Rademacher complexity
quantifies the flexibility of a predictor set and controls generalization error
(
Mohri et al., 2018
)
. We establish the standard definition of Rademacher complexity of a function class, along with a related version stated in the language of supervised learning problems. In what follows, if
\mu
is a measure on a measurable space
X
, let
\mu^{\otimes m}
represent the
m
-fold product measure
\mu\otimes\dots\otimes\mu\in\prob(X^{m})
.

Definition 39
.

Let
m\geq 1
. Given a probability space
(X,\mu)
and a family
\mathcal{F}
of measurable real-valued functions on
X
, the classical
m
th Rademacher complexity of
\mathcal{F}
is given by

R_{m}(\mathcal{F}):=\int\int\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}f(x_{i})\,\mathrm{Rad}^{\otimes m}(d\sigma)\,\mu^{\otimes m}(d\overline{x}),

where
\mathrm{Rad}
is the Rademacher distribution (that is, the uniform distribution on
\{-1,1\}
),
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{m})
,
\overline{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
.
Similarly, the
m
th Rademacher complexity of a problem
P
is given by

R_{m}(P):=\int\int\sup_{h\in H}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}\ell_{h}(x_{i},y_{i})\,\mathrm{Rad}^{\otimes m}(d\sigma)\,\eta^{\otimes m}(d\overline{x}\times d\overline{y}).

That is,
R_{m}(P)
is the
m
th Rademacher complexity of the function class
\{\ell_{h}|h\in H\}
.

The Rademacher complexity of a problem
P
is high if, given a random sample
((x_{i},y_{i}))_{i=1}^{m}
of
m
observations and a vector
\sigma
of random
\pm 1
noise, we can typically find some predictor
h
whose vector of losses
(\ell_{h}(x_{i},y_{i}))_{i=1}^{m}
is highly correlated with the noise
\sigma
. This would suggest that
H
is quite flexible, and possibly that empirical risk minimization over
H
runs the risk of overfitting on a random sample of size
m
.

For a fixed
m
, the Rademacher complexity
R_{m}
need not be stable under the Risk distance.

Example 40
.

For
n\geq 2
, define the problem

P_{n}=([n],\{0,1\},\mathrm{unif}([n])\otimes\delta_{0},\ell,H_{n})

where
[n]:=\{1,\dots,n\}
,
\ell
is the 0-1 loss, and
H_{n}
is the set of indicator functions of singletons
1_{k}:[n]\to\{0,1\}
for all
k\in[n]
. Recall also the one-point problem
P_{\bullet}=P_{\bullet}(0)
from Example
20
.

We claim that
R_{1}(P_{\bullet})=0
,
R_{1}(P_{n})=1/2
for all
n
, but
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P_{n},P_{\bullet})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. This shows that the Rademacher complexity
R_{1}
is not continuous with respect to the Risk distance.
One can easily see that
R_{1}(P_{\bullet})=0
, since the loss function of
P_{\bullet}
is identically 0. We can also easily compute

\displaystyle R_{1}(P_{n})

\displaystyle=\int\left(\frac{1}{2}\sup_{k\in[n]}\ell(1_{k}(x),y)-\frac{1}{2}\inf_{k\in[n]}\ell(1_{k}(x),y)\right)\,(\mathrm{unif}([n])\otimes\delta_{0})(dx\times dy)

\displaystyle=\int\left(\frac{1}{2}(1)-\frac{1}{2}(0)\right)\,(\mathrm{unif}([n])\otimes\delta_{0})(dx\times dy)=\frac{1}{2}.

Meanwhile, since there is only one coupling with the point mass
\delta_{(\bullet,\bullet)}
and only one correspondence with the singleton
\{\id_{\{\bullet\}}\}
, we can compute

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P_{n},P_{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\dis_{P_{n},P_{\bullet}}(H_{n}\times\{\id_{\{\bullet\}}\},\mathrm{unif}([n])\otimes\delta_{0}\otimes\delta_{(\bullet,\bullet)})

\displaystyle=\sup_{k\in[n]}\int|\ell(1_{k}(x),0)-0|\,\mathrm{unif}([n])(dx)

\displaystyle=\sup_{k\in[n]}\int 1_{k}(x)\,\mathrm{unif}([n])(dx)=\sup_{k\in[n]}\frac{1}{n}=\frac{1}{n}.

Hence, even though
P_{n}\to P
in the Risk distance, the Rademacher complexities
R_{1}(P_{n})
do not converge to
R_{1}(P_{\bullet})
.

Example
40
shows that
R_{1}
is not even continuous with respect to the Risk distance. This suggests more generally that we should not hope for straightforward stability of
R_{m}
under the Risk distance when
m
is small. If we instead take
m
to be arbitrarily large, we can still obtain a kind of stability.

Theorem 41

(Stability of Rademacher Complexity)
.

Let
P
and
P^{\prime}
be problems and consider the family

\mathcal{F}:=\left\{\big|\ell_{h}(-,-)-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(-,-)\big|\,\middle|h\in H,h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}\right\}

of functions
X\times Y\times X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Then

\big|R_{m}(P)-R_{m}(P^{\prime})\big|\leq d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})+2R_{m}(\mathcal{F}).

If
P
and
P^{\prime}
have bounded loss functions and
\mathcal{F}
forms a universal Glivenko-Cantelli class, then

\limsup_{m}\big|R_{m}(P)-R_{m}(P^{\prime})\big|\leq d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime}).

The proof can be found in Appendix
A
. The conditions under which
R_{m}(\mathcal{F})\to 0
as
m\to\infty
, and the rate of said convergence, are classical subjects of research in statistical learning theory. Results in this direction typically provide convergence rates of order
O(\sqrt{1/m})

(
Bartlett and Mendelson, 2002
)
or
O(\sqrt{\ln(m)/m})

(
Mohri et al., 2018
, Chapter 3)
under specific assumptions.

One can apply Proposition
13
to produce at a weaker form of Theorem
41
which does not reference the Glivenko-Cantelli property. Specifically, we can replace the universal Glivenko-Cantelli condition Theorem
41
with the following pair of sufficient conditions:

1.

The sets
H
and
H^{\prime}
can be endowed with Polish topologies, and subsequently Borel
\sigma
-algebras, such that the maps
(h,x,y)\mapsto\ell_{h}(x,y)
and
(h^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\mapsto\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
are measurable.

2.

The family of subgraphs
\left\{\mathrm{sg}(g_{h,h^{\prime}})\middle|h\in H,h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}\right\}
(see Definition
12
) has finite VC dimension, where

g_{h,h^{\prime}}(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}):=\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big|.

In fact, we can state an even simpler sufficient condition involving the two problems
P
and
P^{\prime}
separately.

Corollary 42
.

Let
P
and
P^{\prime}
be problems with bounded loss functions. Assume that
H
and
H^{\prime}
are equipped with Polish topologies such that the maps
(h,x,y)\mapsto\ell_{h}(x,y)
and
(h^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\mapsto\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
are measurable.

If both
\left\{\ell_{h}\middle|h\in H\right\}
and
\left\{\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}\middle|h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}\right\}
lie in finite-dimensional subspaces of
L^{1}(X\times Y)
and
L^{1}(X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime})
respectively, then

\limsup_{m}\big|R_{m}(P)-R_{m}(P^{\prime})\big|\leq d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime}).

The subspace condition in Corollary
42
, while simple, may seem strange. We claim the condition is not as odd as it may at first appear.

Example 43
.

Consider the generic linear regression problem
P
from Example
19
. For that problem, the functions
\ell_{h}
are of the form

\ell_{h}(x,y)=(a^{T}x+b-y)^{2}

for
a\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
b\in{\mathbb{R}}
. These functions are polynomials in
x
and
y
of degree at most 2. Hence the collection
\left\{\ell_{h}\middle|h\in H\right\}\subseteq L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}})
lies within the finite-dimensional subspace of polynomials of degree at most 2. Hence
P
satisfies the subspace condition of Corollary
42
.

More generally, any problem

P=({\mathbb{R}}^{n},{\mathbb{R}}^{m},\eta,\ell,H)

will satisfy the subspace condition if
\ell
is a polynomial loss function and
H
is of the form

H=\left\{a_{1}f_{1}(x)+\dots+a_{k}f_{k}(x)\middle|a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\in{\mathbb{R}}\right\}

for some fixed
f_{1},\dots f_{k}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
. This is because the set
\left\{\ell_{h}\middle|h\in H\right\}
will fall within a finite-dimensional space of polynomials in the variables
f_{1},\dots f_{k}
and
y
.

Corollary
42
.

Let

{\mathcal{S}}:=\mathrm{span}(\left\{\ell_{h}(-,-)-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(-,-)\middle|h\in H,h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}\right\}),

where the span is taken in the space of all measurable functions
X\times Y\times X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Consider

\max({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}):=\left\{\max(f,g)\middle|f,g\in{\mathcal{S}}\right\},

where the maximum
\max(f,g)
is taken pointwise. Since
{\mathcal{S}}
is finite-dimensional, it follows
(
Van Der Vaart and Wellner, 1996
, Lemma 2.6.15 and Lemma 2.6.18(ii))
that the collection of subgraphs of functions in
\max({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}})
has finite VC dimension. The set
\left\{g_{h,h^{\prime}}\middle|h\in H,h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}\right\}
lies in
\max({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}})
, so Proposition
13
finishes the proof.
∎

4.3
Stability under Sampling Bias

A fundamental source of error in learning problems of any type is bias in the collection of training data. Suppose
\eta
is the true joint law that we are trying to approximate with a predictor. While we would like to sample from
\eta
directly, an imperfect collection process may over-represent certain regions of the data space. One would hope that minor bias would only have a minor effect on the problem. More formally, we might ask if the Risk distance is stable under small modifications to a problem’s joint law. This section provides conditions for such stability results.

The Risk distance is stable under changes to the joint law with respect to the Wasserstein distance with a certain underlying metric. Under certain conditions, it is also stable with respect to the total variation distance (Definition
9
).
Define the pseudometric
s_{\ell,H}
on
X\times Y
by

s_{\ell,H}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})):=\sup_{h\in H}\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell(h(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\big|.

Proposition 44
.

Let
P
be a problem, and let
P^{\prime}
be a problem that is identical to
P
except possibly for its joint law
\eta^{\prime}
. Then

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})\leq d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{s_{\ell,H}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime}).

Suppose furthermore that the loss function
\ell
is bounded above by some constant
\ell_{\sf{max}}
. Then

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})\leq\ell_{\sf{max}}d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime}).

Proof.

One can bound
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})
by choosing the diagonal correspondence between
H
and itself to get

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}\sup_{h\in H}\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell(h(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\big|\,\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}\int\sup_{h\in H}\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell(h(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\big|\,\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})=d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{s_{\ell,H}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime}),

proving the first part of the proposition. Now, if
\ell
is bounded by
\ell_{\sf{max}}
then
s_{\ell,H}
is bounded by
\ell_{\sf{max}}d
, where
d
is the discrete metric. Hence

d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{s_{\ell,H}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\leq d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{\ell_{\sf{max}}d}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=\ell_{\sf{max}}d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})

as desired.
∎

In the presence of sampling bias, the data collection process gives inordinate weight to some regions of the data space
X\times Y
at the cost of other regions. We can think of this biased data collection as sampling not from the true probability measure
\eta
, but instead from a scaled probability measure given by

f\eta(A):=\int_{A}f(x,y)\,\eta(dx\times dy)

for all measurable
A
, where
f:X\times Y\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a density function with respect to
\eta
, meaning
f
is a measurable function with
\int f(x,y)\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)=1
. Here
f
is larger than 1 in regions of
X\times Y
that are overrepresented by the data collection process, and less than 1 in regions underrepresented.

In extreme cases of sampling bias, some regions of the data space
X\times Y
may be completely missing from the training data. In this case, instead of being sampled from the true joint law
\eta
, the data is sampled from some subset
A\subseteq X\times Y
with
\eta(A)>0
according to the probability measure

\eta|_{A}(B):=\frac{1}{\eta(A)}\eta(A\cap B).

Using Proposition
44
, we can show that
d_{\mathrm{R}}
is stable under the above formulations of sampling bias.

Corollary 45

(Stability Under Sampling Bias)
.

Let
P
be a problem.

1.

Let
f:X\times Y\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a density function with respect to
\eta
. Define

P_{f}:=(X,Y,f\eta,\ell,H).

Then

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P_{f})\leq\frac{1}{2}\int\big|1-f(x,y)\big|\,\eta(dx{\times}dy).

2.

Let

A\subseteq X\times Y
be a measurable subset with
\eta(A)>0
. Define

P|_{A}:=(X,Y,\eta|_{A},\ell,H).

Then

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P|_{A})\leq 1-\eta(A).

Proof.

First recall that

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=\sup_{B\subseteq X\times Y}\eta(B)-f\eta(B)=\sup_{B\subseteq X\times Y}f\eta(B)-\eta(B),

where the infima range over all measurable subsets of
X\times Y
. Then

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sup_{B\subseteq X\times Y}\eta(B)-f\eta(B)+\sup_{B\subseteq X\times Y}f\eta(B)-\eta(B)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sup_{B\subseteq X\times Y}\int_{B}1-f(x,y)\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)+\sup_{B\subseteq X\times Y}\int_{B}f(x,y)-1\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{\{f<1\}}1-f(x,y)\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)+\int_{\{f>1\}}f(x,y)-1\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\big|1-f(x,y)\big|\,\eta(dx{\times}dy).

To finish the proof, apply part (1) when
f=\frac{1}{\eta(A)}1_{A}
.
∎

4.4
Stability under Sampling Noise

Another source of error in sampling is the presence of noise in the process of collecting data. While we would like to sample from
\eta
directly, our data is rarely collected with perfect fidelity. One would hope that a small amount of noise would not change the problem much. In this section, we model noise with a
noise kernel
and show that we can often guarantee that the Risk distance is stable under the application of such noise.

First we consider the case where the loss function
\ell
is bounded by a metric on
Y
. Our bounds are in terms of a generalization of the Wasserstein metric for Markov kernels.

Definition 46
.

Let
X
be a measure space and let
M:X\to\prob(Y)
,
N:X\to\prob(Z)
be Markov kernels. A
coupling
between
M
and
N
is a Markov kernel
\tau:X\to\prob(Y\times Z)
such that for a.e.
x\in X
,

\displaystyle(\pi_{1})_{\sharp}(\tau(x))

\displaystyle=M(x),

\displaystyle(\pi_{2})_{\sharp}(\tau(x))

\displaystyle=N(x).

Just as we use
\Pi(\mu,\nu)
to denote the set of all couplings of probability measures
\mu
and
\nu
, we use
\Pi(M,N)
to denote the set of all couplings of Markov kernels
M
and
N
.

Note that if
X
is a single point, we recover the classical definition of a coupling of probability measures.

Definition 47

(Wasserstein Distance for Markov Kernels)
.

Let
(X,\mu)
be a measure space and let
(Y,d_{Y})
be a metric space. Let
M,N:X\to\prob(Y)
be a pair of Markov kernels. For
1\leq p<\infty
, the
p
-Wasserstein distance between
M
and
N
is given by

d_{\mathrm{W},p}(M,N):=\left(\inf_{\tau\in\Pi(M,N)}\int_{X}\int_{Y\times Y}d_{Y}^{p}(y,y^{\prime})\tau(x)(dy\times dy^{\prime})\mu(dx)\right)^{1/p}.

Definitions
46
and
47
are due to
Patterson (2020, Defs 5.1 and 5.2)
. Patterson notes that terminology conflicts between “products” and “couplings” of Markov kernels are present in the literature.
As with the Wasserstein distance, we will have little use for
d_{\mathrm{W},p}
for
p\neq 1
, and so establish the convention that the
Wasserstein distance for Markov kernels
refers to
d_{\mathrm{W},1}
.
An alternative, equivalent definition of the metric
d_{\mathrm{W},1}
may be more intuitive:

d_{\mathrm{W},1}(M,N)=\int_{X}d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{d_{Y}}(M(x),N(x))\,\mu(dx).

Equivalence of these definitions was noted by
Patterson (2020)
, and follows from results in the theory of optimal transport
(
Villani, 2008
, Corollary 5.22)
. A distance based on a generalization of the above alternative definition was independently discovered and studied by
Kitagawa and Takatsu (2023)
under the name “disintegrated Monge-Kantorovich distance”.

We are abusing notation by using the same symbol for the Wasserstein distances between measures and between Markov kernels. We justify this by noting that the former is a special case of the latter. Indeed, recall that if
\mu,\nu\in\prob(X)
are probability measures on a metric space
X
, we can think of
\mu
and
\nu
as Markov kernels from the one-point measure space
(\{\bullet\},\delta_{\bullet})
. The Wasserstein distance between these Markov kernels then agrees with the Wasserstein distance between the measures
\mu
and
\nu
.

The Wasserstein distance for Markov kernels also generalizes the
L^{1}
distance on measurable functions. Indeed, if we have measurable functions
f,g:X\to Y
, then the distance
d_{\mathrm{W},1}(\delta_{f},\delta_{g})
between the induced deterministic Markov kernels is just the
L^{1}
distance between
f
and
g
.

A simple kind of noise can be modeled with a Markov kernel
M:Y\to\prob(Y)
. We interpret this noise as follows. When we make an observation that would have label
y\in Y
, we instead observe a random corrupted label with law
M(y)
. More specifically, let
P
be a problem whose joint law has
X
-marginal
\alpha
and
X
-disintegration
\beta:X\to\prob(Y)
. Without the presence of noise, an observation has a random input component
x
chosen with law
\alpha
, and a random label chosen with law
\beta(x)
. The presence of noise represented by
M
creates a new problem where the labels are instead chosen according to the law
(\beta\cdot M)(x)
. In other words, we get a problem with the same
X
-marginal
\alpha
, but with disintegration
\beta^{\prime}:X\to\prob(Y)
given by

\beta^{\prime}(x):=(\beta\cdot M)(x).

We might call this type of noise “
X
-independent
Y
-noise,” (otherwise known as “simple label noise”) since the noise
M
is applied in the
Y
direction, and the same noise kernel
M
is applied for all
x\in X
.

A more general kind of noise would allow for dependence on
X
. Instead of a Markov kernel
M:Y\to\prob(Y)
, we model our noise as a Markov kernel
N:X\times Y\to\prob(Y)
. We write
N_{x}
to mean the Markov kernel
N(x,-):Y\to\prob(Y)
. In the presence of this kind of noise, the problem
P
becomes a new problem
P^{\prime}
with the same
X
-marginal
\alpha
, but
X
-disintegration
\beta^{\prime}
now given by

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}(x):=(\beta\cdot N_{x})(x).

We might call this kind of noise “
X
-dependent
Y
-noise”.

Consider a noise kernel
N=\delta_{\pi_{2}}
, which is the Markov kernel sending
(x,y)
to the point mass
\delta_{y}
. Then for all
x\in X
, we have
N_{x}(y)=\delta_{y}
and hence
\beta\cdot N_{x}=\beta
. In other words,
N
represents no noise at all, and applying
N
to a problem would leave the problem unchanged. Our next stability result states that, if any kernel
N
is close to this “no noise” case under
d_{\mathrm{W},1}
, then applying
N
will not move the problem much in the Risk distance, provided that
\ell
is well-controlled by some metric on
Y
.

Lemma 48
.

Suppose that
P
and
P^{\prime}
are problems given by

P=(X,Y,\eta,\ell,H)

P^{\prime}=(X,Y,\eta^{\prime},\ell,H)

where
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
have the same
X
-marginal
\alpha\in\prob(X)
, but different disintegrations along
X
, called
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
respectively. Suppose also that
Y
is equipped with a metric
d_{Y}
with respect to which
\ell
is Lipschitz in the second argument, meaning there is a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\big|\ell(z,y)-\ell(z,y^{\prime})\big|\leq Cd_{Y}(y,y^{\prime})

for all
y,y^{\prime},z\in Y
. Then

d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})\leq Cd_{\mathrm{W},1}^{d_{Y}}(\beta,\beta^{\prime}).

Theorem 49

(Stability under
X
-dependent
Y
-noise)
.

Let
P
be a problem whose output space
Y
is equipped with a metric
d_{Y}
, with respect to which the loss function
\ell
is
C
-Lipschitz in the second argument. Let
P^{\prime}
be the problem obtained by applying the noise kernel
N:X\times Y\to\prob(Y)
to
P
. Then

d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})\leq Cd_{\mathrm{W},1}^{d_{Y}}(N,\delta_{\pi_{2}}).

The proofs are relegated to Appendix
A
.
We illustrate Theorem
49
with an example regarding label noise in binary classification.

Example 50
.

Let
P=(X,\{0,1\},\eta,\ell,H)
be a binary classification problem where
\ell
is the 0-1 loss
\ell(y,y^{\prime})=1_{y\neq y^{\prime}}
. Suppose we apply noise to
P
by taking each observation
(x,y)
and doing the following:

•

With probability
\varepsilon
, re-assign its label
y
uniformly at random.

•

With probability
(1-\varepsilon)
, leave the label
y
as it is.

Through this process we are effectively applying the noise kernel

N_{\varepsilon}:=\varepsilon u+(1-\varepsilon)\delta_{\pi_{2}},

where
u
is the uniform measure on
\{0,1\}
. Let
P^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
be the problem
P
with the noise kernel
N_{\varepsilon}
applied.
Since the 0-1 loss is a metric, we can apply Theorem
49
with
d_{Y}=\ell
and
C=1
to get

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathrm{W},1}(\varepsilon u+(1-\varepsilon)\delta_{\pi_{2}},\delta_{\pi_{2}})

\displaystyle=\int d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{\ell}(\varepsilon u+(1-\varepsilon)\delta_{y},\delta_{y})\,\eta(dx{\times}dy).

Recall (Section
2.4
) that when the underlying metric is the discrete metric, the Wasserstein distance is the same as the total variation distance. Furthermore, one can show that
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\varepsilon u+(1-\varepsilon)\delta_{y},\delta_{y})=\varepsilon d_{\mathrm{TV}}(u,\delta_{y})=\varepsilon/2
. Hence we get

d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})\leq\varepsilon/2.

One could generalize further; suppose that the probability
\varepsilon
of reassignment
depends on
x
. That is, instead of selecting a constant
\varepsilon
, we select a measurable function
\varepsilon:X\to[0,1]
. Then, for an observation
(x,y)
, with probability
\varepsilon(x)
we re-assign
y
uniformly at random. Then the same argument above instead gives us the bound

d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})\leq\frac{1}{2}\int\varepsilon(x)\alpha(dx),

where
\alpha
is the
X
-marginal of
\eta
. That is, the risk distance between
P
and the noised problem
P^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
is bounded by half the
average
value of
\varepsilon(x)
.

In Section
6.5
, we will consider an even more general noise model, allowing for noise in the
X
and
Y
directions simultaneously. We will show that a modification of the Risk distance, called the
L^{p}
-Risk distance, is stable under such noise in Theorem
91
, which is analogous to Theorem
49
.

4.5
Stability under Modification of Predictor Set

We have shown that
d_{\mathrm{R}}
is stable under changes to the loss function and certain changes to
\eta
. We round out this section by demonstrating stability of
d_{\mathrm{R}}
under changes to the predictor set of a problem.

Figure 3:
Distance between
H
and
H_{X,Y}
; see Example
52
.

To state such a stability result, we first need a way to quantify changes to the predictor set. The most salient aspect of a predictor
h
is its performance with respect to the chosen loss function. In other words, more crucial than the predictor
h
itself is the function
\ell_{h}(x,y):=\ell(h(x),y)
. With this perspective in mind, we can map
H
into
L^{1}(\eta)
via the map
h\mapsto\ell_{h}
, and compare predictors using the
L^{1}
norm there. That is, we compare two predictors
h
and
h^{\prime}
via the quantity

\displaystyle d_{\ell,\eta}(h,h^{\prime}):=\|\ell_{h}-\ell_{h^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}(\eta)}=\int\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell_{h^{\prime}}(x,y)\big|\,\eta(dx{\times}dy).

This defines a pseudometric on the set of all measurable functions
h:X\to Y
for which
\|\ell_{h}\|_{L^{1}(\eta)}<\infty
, which any predictor satisfies since the definition of a problem mandates that predictors have finite risk. The map
h\mapsto\ell_{h}
identifies two predictors
h
and
h^{\prime}
if they
\eta
-almost always incur the same loss on a random observation in
X\times Y
.

We can now state a stability theorem in terms of the Hausdorff distance on predictor sets with underlying metric
d_{\ell,H}
, i.e. the metric

d_{\mathrm{H}}^{d_{\ell,\eta}}(H,H^{\prime})=\inf_{R\in{\mathcal{C}}(H,H^{\prime})}\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\|\ell_{h}-\ell_{h^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}(\eta)}.

Theorem 51
.

Let
P
be a problem, and let
P^{\prime}
be a problem that is identical to
P
except possibly for the predictor set
H^{\prime}
. Then

d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})\leq d_{\mathrm{H}}^{d_{\ell,\eta}}\left(H,H^{\prime}\right).

Example 52
.

Note that if
P=(X,Y,\eta,\ell,H)
and
P^{\prime}=(X,Y,\eta,\ell,H_{X,Y})
, where
H_{X,Y}
stands for the set of all measurable functions from
X
to
Y
, Theorem
37
implies that

{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(P)\leq{\mathcal{B}}(P)\leq{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(P)+\sup_{h^{\prime}\in H_{X,Y}}\inf_{h\in H}d_{\ell,\eta}(h,h^{\prime}).

The right-hand side contains the worst case
d_{\ell,\eta}
distance between an arbitrary measurable function
h^{\prime}
and the (constrained) predictor set
H
. In other words, this quantity expresses how far
H
is from the superclass
H_{X,Y}
and therefore controls the
approximation error
: the difference between the constrained and unconstrained Bayes risks; see Figure
3
.

Theorem
51
.

We bound
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})
by choosing the diagonal coupling between
\eta
and itself. That is,

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{R\in{\mathcal{C}}(H,H^{\prime})}\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell(h^{\prime}(x),y)\big|\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)

\displaystyle=\inf_{R\in{\mathcal{C}}(H,H^{\prime})}\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\|\ell_{h}-\ell_{h^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}(\eta)}=d_{\mathrm{H}}^{d_{\ell,\eta}}(H,H^{\prime}),

which is the desired bound.
∎

We illustrate the metric
d_{\mathrm{H}}^{d_{\ell,\eta}}
and Theorem
51
with a toy example.

Example 53
.

Let
P=({\mathbb{R}}^{2},\{0,1\},\eta,\ell,H)
be the classification problem from Example
18
. Recall that the predictors in
H
were the functions of the form

\displaystyle h_{a,b,c}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\begin{cases}1&ax_{1}+bx_{2}\geq c\\
0&ax_{1}+bx_{2}<c\end{cases}.

We modify
H
by adding to each predictor a small area on which they always predict class 1. That is, we define a new predictor set
H^{\prime}_{t}:=\left\{h^{\prime}_{a,b,c,t}\middle|a,b,c\in{\mathbb{R}}\right\}
where
h^{\prime}_{a,b,c,t}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to\{0,1\}
is given by

\displaystyle h^{\prime}_{a,b,c,t}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\begin{cases}1&x\in B_{t}(0)\\
h_{a,b,c}(x)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

Here
B_{t}(0)
represents the ball of radius
t
centered at 0.
We can construct a correspondence between
H
and
H^{\prime}_{t}
by coupling each
h_{a,b,c}
with
h^{\prime}_{a,b,c,t}
. That is, we define

R:=\left\{(h_{a,b,c},h^{\prime}_{a,b,c,t})\middle|a,b,c\in{\mathbb{R}}\right\}.

Then
R\in{\mathcal{C}}(H,H^{\prime}_{t})
, so we can write the bound

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{H}}^{d_{\ell,\eta}}(H,H^{\prime}_{t})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(h_{a,b,c},h^{\prime}_{a,b,c,t})\in R}\|\ell_{h_{a,b,c}}-\ell_{h_{a,b,c,t}}\|_{L^{1}(\eta)}

\displaystyle=\sup_{a,b,c\in{\mathbb{R}}}\int\big|\ell(h_{a,b,c}(x),y)-\ell(h_{a,b,c,t}(x),y)\big|\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)

\displaystyle=\sup_{a,b,c\in{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{B_{t}(0)\times\{0,1\}}\big|\ell(h_{a,b,c}(x),y)-\ell(h_{a,b,c,t}(x),y)\big|\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)

\displaystyle\leq\eta(B_{t}(0)\times\{0,1\}).

Furthermore, assuming
\eta({0}\times\{0,1\})=0
, we can take the limit as
t\to 0
to get
\lim_{t\to 0}d_{\mathrm{H}}^{d_{\ell,\eta}}(H,H^{\prime}_{t})=0
. If we define
P_{t}:=({\mathbb{R}}^{2},\{0,1\},\eta,\ell,H^{\prime}_{t})
for all
t>0
, By Theorem
51
,
P_{t}
converges to
P
under the Risk distance as
t\to 0
.

We now provide a more serious application of Theorem
51
. Different classes of predictors are often compared in the context of
universal approximation theorems
, which generally state that a certain kind of function can be approximated arbitrarily well using a certain class of model. Using Theorem
51
, one can translate universal approximation theorems into statements about the risk distance. While there are many universal approximation theorems
(
Pinkus, 1999
)
, we will demonstrate with a classical example
(
Leshno et al., 1993
, see)
.

Proposition 54
.

Let
\phi:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a continuous non-polynomial function. Let
P=(X,{\mathbb{R}},\eta,\ell,H)
be a problem where

•

X
is a compact subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
,

•

\eta
is any probability distribution on
X
,

•

\ell
is a uniformly continuous loss function, and

•

H
is the set of all functions
X\to{\mathbb{R}}
which can come from a feedforward network with a single hidden layer of arbitrary width and with activation function
\phi
. That is,
H
is the set of all functions of the form

(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{M}a_{i}\phi\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}w_{ij}x_{j}+b_{i}\right)

where the parameters
a_{i}
,
w_{ij}
, and
b_{j}
range over
{\mathbb{R}}
, and
M
ranges over the positive integers.

Let
P^{\prime}
be the same problem, but with predictor set
H^{\prime}
the set of all continuous functions
X\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Then
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})=0
.

Proof.

Let
h^{\prime}:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
be continuous and let
\varepsilon>0
be arbitrary. By uniform continuity of
\ell
, there exists a
\delta>0
such that, if
|y-y^{\prime}|<\delta
, then
|\ell(y,y^{\prime\prime})-\ell(y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})|<\varepsilon
for any
y^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. By
(
Leshno et al., 1993
, Thm 1)
, there exists an
h\in H
with
|h(x)-h^{\prime}(x)|<\delta
for all
x\in X
. Then

\displaystyle d_{\ell,\eta}(h,h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell(h^{\prime}(x),y)\big|\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)\leq\int\varepsilon\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)=\varepsilon.

Since
H\subseteq H^{\prime}
, this implies the Hausdorff distance
d_{H}^{d_{\ell,\eta}}(H,H^{\prime})
is less than
\varepsilon
. By Theorem
51
,
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})<\varepsilon
as well.
∎

In other words, under certain conditions, the risk distance does not distinguish between a model and the class of functions in which it is dense.

5
Geometry of the Space of Problems

We now shift our attention from stability properties of
d_{\mathrm{R}}
to its geometric properties. We will explore geodesics and their connection to optimal couplings and correspondences. We also show that classification problems (that is, those with finite output spaces) are dense in a larger class of problems. We will use this density to study the effect of limited sampling by studying the convergence of “empirical problems,” which are problems whose joint laws are given by empirical measures gleaned from finite random samples.

P
P’

Figure 4:
A simple depiction of two problems
P
and
P^{\prime}
in the space of problems, joined by a geodesic.

5.1
Geodesics

We begin our exploration of the geometry of the space of problems by searching for geodesics. A
geodesic
in a metric space
(X,d_{X})
is a continuous function from the unit interval
f:[0,1]\to X
such that, for all
s,t\in[0,1]
,

d_{X}(f(t),f(s))=d_{X}(f(0),f(1))|s-t|.

Some would call such an
f
a “shortest path”
(
Burago et al., 2001
, Chapter 2.5)
and reserve the term “geodesic” for a path that
locally
satisfies the above property. However, the above definition is standard in the field of optimal transport
(
Ambrosio et al., 2021
, Section 9.2, Eq. 9.4)
.

While valuable in illuminating the geometry of a space, geodesics are not solely of geometric interest. A geodesic between two points gives us a way to interpolate or average between them. If a family
\{P_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of problems defines a geodesic in the space of problems, then
P_{1/2}
could reasonably be considered an average of
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
. If we would rather take a weighted average, we could choose smaller or larger values of
t
to give more weight to
P_{0}
or to
P_{1}
respectively.

We will show that geodesics can be supplied by certain couplings and correspondences.

Definition 55
.

Let
P
and
P^{\prime}
be problems.
If there exists a coupling
\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
and a correspondence
R\in{\mathcal{C}}(H,H^{\prime})
such that

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})=\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R,\gamma),

then we call
\gamma
an
optimal coupling
and
R
an
optimal correspondence
for
P
and
P^{\prime}
. That is,
\gamma
and
R
are called optimal if they achieve the infima in the definition of
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})
.

In Section
5.2
, we will explore sufficient conditions for the existence of optimal couplings and correspondences. Presently, we motivate our interest in optimal couplings and correspondences by showing that they are closely related to the geometry of the space of problems; optimal couplings and correspondences provide us with explicit geodesics in the space of problems.

Theorem 56
.

Suppose
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
admit an optimal coupling
\gamma
and an optimal correspondence
R
. Then the following family of problems is a geodesic between
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
:

P_{t}:=(X_{0}\times X_{1},Y_{0}\times Y_{1},\gamma,\ell_{t},R_{\times})

for all
t\in[0,1]
, where

•

\ell_{t}((y_{0},y_{1}),(y^{\prime}_{0},y^{\prime}_{1})):=(1-t)\ell_{0}(y_{0},y^{\prime}_{0})+t\ell_{1}(y_{1},y^{\prime}_{1})
.

•

R_{\times}:=\{h_{0}\times h_{1}|h_{0}\in H_{0},h_{1}\in H_{1}\}
.

It may at first appear that we have given
P_{0}
two conflicting definitions in the above statement, once as the given

P_{0}:=(X_{0},Y_{0},\eta_{0},\ell_{0},H_{0})

and again by setting
t=0
in our parameterized family, yielding

P_{0}:=(X_{0}\times X_{1},Y_{0}\times Y_{1},\gamma,\ell_{0},R_{\times}).

However, the latter problem is a simulation of the former via the projection maps
X_{0}\times X_{1}\to X_{0}
and
Y_{0}\times Y_{1}\to Y_{0}
. Hence the two problems are distance zero apart under the Risk distance and can be safely identified. A similar line of thought applies to
P_{1}
.

The proof that this family is a geodesic is very similar to the proofs of analogous results for the Gromov-Hausdorff and Gromov-Wasserstein distances
(
Chowdhury and Mémoli, 2018
;
Sturm, 2023
)
.

Proof.

Define
C:=d_{\mathrm{R}}(P_{0},P_{1})
. Let
0\leq s\leq t\leq 1
. If we can show

d_{\mathrm{R}}(P_{s},P_{t})\leq C(t-s),

then equality will follow. Indeed, if the inequality were strict for some
0\leq s_{0}\leq t_{0}\leq 1
, then we could use the triangle inequality to write

\displaystyle C=d_{\mathrm{R}}(P_{0},P_{1})

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathrm{R}}(P_{0},P_{s_{0}})+d_{\mathrm{R}}(P_{s_{0}},P_{t_{0}})+d_{\mathrm{R}}(P_{t_{0}},P_{1})

\displaystyle<s_{0}C+(t_{0}-s_{0})C+(1-s_{0})C=C,

which is a contradiction.

Now, we can bound
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P_{s},P_{t})
by selecting the diagonal coupling from
\Pi(\gamma,\gamma)
and diagonal correspondence from
{\mathcal{C}}(R_{\times},R_{\times})
to get

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P_{s},P_{t})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{h_{0}\times h_{1}\in R_{\times}}\int\big|\ell_{s}((h_{0}(x_{0}),h_{1}(x_{1})),(y_{0},y_{1}))-\ell_{t}((h_{0}(x_{0}),h_{1}(x_{1})),(y_{0},y_{1}))\big|\,\gamma(dx_{0}{\times}dy_{0}{\times}dx_{1}{\times}dy_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{h_{0}\times h_{1}\in R_{\times}}\int\big|(t-s)\big(\ell_{0}(h_{0}(x_{0}),y_{0})+\ell_{1}(h_{1}(x_{1}),y_{1})\big)\big|\,\gamma(dx_{0}{\times}dy_{0}{\times}dx_{1}{\times}dy_{1})

\displaystyle=(t-s)\sup_{(h_{0},h_{1})\in R}\int\big|\ell_{0}(h_{0}(x_{0}),y_{0})+\ell_{1}(h_{1}(x_{1}),y_{1})\big|\,\gamma(dx_{0}{\times}dy_{0}{\times}dx_{1}{\times}dy_{1})

\displaystyle=(t-s)C.

The last equality is because
\gamma
and
R
are an optimal coupling and correspondence of
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
.
∎

5.2
Existence of Optimal Couplings and Correspondences

Motivated by Theorem
56
, we search for conditions under which optimal couplings and correspondences exist. We will achieve this by imposing some continuity and compactness conditions on our problems.

Definition 57
.

A problem
P
is called
continuous
if, for all
h\in H
,
\ell_{h}
is continuous
\eta
-almost everywhere.

Note that the above condition applies to many relevant problems. A typical linear regression problem will be continuous. For instance, the linear regression problem in Example
19
is continuous, since the loss is continuous, as is every predictor. Under reasonable assumptions, a classification problem can be continuous as well; one only needs the possible decision boundaries to each have measure zero in the
X
-marginal. Take the classification problem
P
in Example
18
. In that problem,
\ell_{h}
is discontinuous for every predictor
h
. However, if we assume that any line in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
has measure zero with respect to the
X
-marginal of
\eta
(as would be the case if the
X
-marginal of
\eta
is given by a continuous probability distribution), then any linear decision boundary has measure zero. Consequently,
\ell_{h}
will be continuous (indeed, constant)
\eta
-almost everywhere.

Let
P,P^{\prime}
be continuous problems. Recall that the risk distortion is the function

\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}:{\mathcal{C}}(H,H^{\prime})\times\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\to\mathbb{R}

\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R,\gamma):=\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\int\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big|\,\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime}).

We would like to discuss the potential continuity of this function, and hence require a suitable topology on its domain. We endow
\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
with the weak topology. We give the set
\left\{\ell_{h}\middle|h\in H\right\}
the
L^{1}(\eta)
topology, and endow
H
with the initial topology induced by the map
h\mapsto\ell_{h}
. Equivalently, we endow
H
with the pseudometric
d_{\ell,\eta}
defined in Section
4.5
and similarly equip
H^{\prime}
with
d_{\ell^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}
.
We then endow
H\times H^{\prime}
with the product metric
\delta((g,g^{\prime}),(h,h^{\prime})):=\max\{d_{\ell,\eta}(g,h),d_{\ell^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(g^{\prime},h^{\prime})\}
, and place the resulting Hausdorff distance
d_{\mathrm{H}}^{\delta}
on
{\mathcal{C}}(H,H^{\prime})
.

Define

\widetilde{\dis}_{P,P^{\prime}}:H\times H^{\prime}\times\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\to{\mathbb{R}}

\widetilde{\dis}_{P,P^{\prime}}(h,h^{\prime},\gamma):=\int\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big|\,\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime}).

Hence

\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R,\gamma)=\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\widetilde{\dis}_{P,P^{\prime}}(h,h^{\prime},\gamma).

We proceed with a sequence of lemmas. We begin by showing that, under certain conditions,
\widetilde{\dis}_{P,P^{\prime}}
is lower semi-continuous, then leverage this result to obtain lower semi-continuity of
\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}
. Recall that a function
f:S\to{\mathbb{R}}
on a topological space
S
is
lower semi-continuous
if, for all
x_{0}\in S
, we have
\liminf_{x\to x_{0}}f(x)\geq f(x_{0})
. Lower semi-continuity is a useful notion because such functions satisfy a limited version of the extreme value theorem. Specifically, a lower semi-continuous function on a compact space achieves its minimum.

Lemma 58
.

Let
P
and
P^{\prime}
be continuous problems. Then
\widetilde{\dis}_{P,P^{\prime}}
is lower semi-continuous.

Lemma 59
.

Let
P
and
P^{\prime}
be continuous problems. Then
\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}
is lower semi-continuous.

Using the fact that a lower semi-continuous function on a compact set achieves its minimum, we can prove the following Theorem.

Theorem 60
.

If
P
and
P^{\prime}
are continuous problems with compact predictor sets, then there exists an optimal coupling and correspondence for
P
and
P^{\prime}
. That is, there exists
R\in{\mathcal{C}}(H,H^{\prime})
and
\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
such that

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})=\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R,\gamma).

The proofs can be found in Appendix
A
.
In turn, Theorem
60
and the characterization of weak isomorphism provided by Theorem
29
give us conditions under which weak isomorphism is equivalent to being Risk distance zero apart.

Corollary 61
.

If
P
and
P^{\prime}
are continuous problems with compact predictor sets, then
P
and
P^{\prime}
are weakly isomorphic if and only if
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})=0
.

5.3
Density of Classification Problems

One benefit of equipping a set with a pseudometric is that, in return, we receive a notion of convergence. One can ask whether a sequence of points converges, how quickly it does so, and what the limit might be. Density results pave the way for limiting arguments. It is often possible, by a limiting argument, to transfer knowledge about a “simple” dense subspace to the space as a whole.

In this section, we show that classification problems (that is, problems whose output space is a finite collection of labels) are dense in a broader class of functions under
d_{\mathrm{R}}
. We will leverage this information in Section
5.4
to prove the main result of the section: the convergence of empirical problems (Theorem
69
).

Definition 62

(Classification Problem)
.

A problem is called a
classification problem
if its response space is finite. A problem is called
compact
if its response space is compact and its loss function is continuous.

A classification problem is so called because we can think of its response space as a finite collection of labels. In Appendix
B
, we characterize the problems that are weakly isomorphic to classification problems.

\qquad\underset{\text{Coarsening}}{\scalebox{4}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}\qquad

Figure 5:
A depiction of the coarsening process in Example
64
. On the left is a heat map depicting the joint law
\eta
of a problem
P
whose input and output spaces are both the interval
X=Y=[0,3]
. We consider the partition
Q=\{[0,1),[1,2),[2,3]\}
of
Y=[0,3]
. The coarsened problem
P_{Q}:=(X,Q,\eta_{Q},\ell_{Q},H_{Q})
has three points in the output space
Q
, represented by the vertical axis. The joint law
\eta_{Q}
is then a probability measure on three copies of
[0,3]
. We depict
\eta_{Q}
on the right with density plots of the three class-conditional probability measures.

Given a compact problem
P
, we can produce a related classification problem
P_{Q}
by clustering the response space into a finite partition
Q
and applying a procedure we call
coarsening
.
The definition of a coarsening makes use of a certain map associated to a partition. Given a set
A
and a partition
Q
of
A
, consider the map
\pi_{Q}:A\to Q
which sends each
a\in A
to the unique partition block
q\in Q
which contains
a
. That is,
\pi_{Q}
is defined such that
a\in\pi_{Q}(a)
for all
a\in A
. This map
\pi_{Q}
is often called the
quotient map
associated to
Q
.

Definition 63

(Coarsening)
.

Let
P
be a problem, let
Q
be a finite partition of
Y
into measurable sets, and let
\pi_{Q}:Y\to Q
be the quotient map associated to
Q
.

The
coarsening of
P
with respect to
Q
is the
classification
problem

P_{Q}=(X,Q,\eta_{Q},\ell_{Q},H_{Q})

where

•

\eta_{Q}:=(\id_{X}\times\pi_{Q})_{\sharp}\eta

•

\ell_{Q}(q,q^{\prime}):=\sup_{y\in q,y^{\prime}\in q^{\prime}}\ell(y,y^{\prime})
,

•

H_{Q}:=\left\{\pi_{Q}\circ h\middle|h\in H\right\}
.

Note that if
Q
is a finite partition of
Y
, then
Q
is itself a finite set of partition blocks. The response space
Q
of the coarsened problem
P_{Q}
is a finite set with one element for each block of the partition
Q
, and hence
P_{Q}
is indeed a classification problem.

Example 64
.

An example of the coarsening procedure is depicted in Figure
5
. We imagine a problem

P=([0,3],[0,3],\eta,\ell,H)

where
\eta
is as pictured,
\ell(y_{1},y_{2}):=|y_{1}-y_{2}|
is the Euclidean distance, and
H
is the set of increasing continuous bijections
[0,3]\to[0,3]
. We choose the partition
Q=\{[0,1),[1,2),[2,3]\}
of
P
and consider the coarsened problem

P_{Q}=([0,3],Q,\eta_{Q},\ell_{Q},H_{Q}).

Then
P_{Q}
is a classification problem with three points in its output space
Q=\{q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\}
, where
q_{1}:=[0,1),q_{2}:=[1,2),
and
q_{3}:=[2,3]
.
Then
\eta_{Q}
is a probability measure on
[0,3]\times Q
, a space which consists of three line segments:
[0,3]\times\{q_{1}\}
,
[0,3]\times\{q_{2}\}
, and
[0,3]\times\{q_{3}\}
.

The coarsened loss

\ell_{Q}(q_{i},q_{j}):=\sup_{y\in q_{i},y^{\prime}\in q_{j}}\ell(y,y^{\prime})\,\,\mbox{for $i,j=1,2,3$},

can be written in the form of a table:

\ell_{Q}

q_{1}

q_{2}

q_{3}

q_{1}

1

2

3

q_{2}

2

1

2

q_{3}

3

2

1

The predictor set
H_{Q}
is
\left\{\pi_{Q}\circ h\middle|h\in H\right\}
, where
\pi_{Q}:Y\to Q
is the quotient map. Then
H_{Q}
can also be written as the set of all functions
g:[0,3]\to Q
of the form

g(t)=\begin{cases}q_{1}&t<a\\
q_{2}&a\leq t<b\\
q_{3}&b\leq t\end{cases}

for all
0<a\leq b<3
.

Coarsening is reminiscent of the “probing reduction”
(
Langford and Zadrozny, 2005
)
, which takes a probability estimation problem and solves it with an ensemble of classifier learners, hence “reducing” the problem of probability estimation to the problem of binary classification. Indeed, one can view the probing reduction as a practical method of approximating a problem whose output space is
Y=[0,1]
by iteratively constructing finer and finer partitions of
Y
and solving the resulting coarsened problems with binary classifiers.

Theorem 65
.

Let
P
be a compact problem. For any

\varepsilon>0
, there is a partition
Q
of the response space such that

d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P_{Q})<\varepsilon.

The proof is relegated to Appendix
A
.

Remark 66
.

One notable feature of the proof of Theorem
65
is that the partition
Q
provided by the theorem does not depend on the joint law
\eta
of
P
. More precisely, suppose
P
is a problem. For any probability measure
\eta^{\prime}\in\prob(X\times Y)
, we can create a new problem

P_{\eta^{\prime}}:=(X,Y,\eta^{\prime},\ell,H).

The proof of Theorem
65
shows that, not only can we find a partition
Q
of
Y
such that
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P_{Q})<\varepsilon
, but we can choose
Q
such that for all
\eta^{\prime}\in\prob(X\times Y)
, we have
d_{\mathrm{R}}((P_{\eta^{\prime}})_{Q},P_{\eta^{\prime}})<\varepsilon
. We will use this stronger fact to prove Theorem
69
.

Theorem
65
immediately gives us a density result.

Corollary 67

(Classification Density Theorem)
.

Under
d_{\mathrm{R}}
, the set of classification problems is dense in the space of compact problems.

5.4
Convergence of Empirical Problems

With the density of classification problems in hand, we are ready to examine the effect that limited sampling can have on a problem. One often does not have direct knowledge of the underlying joint law from which data is sampled; one only has access to a finite data set. In terms of our supervised learning problems, instead of accessing the joint law
\eta
of a problem
P
directly, we can only access the empirical measure
\eta_{n}
of
\eta
. Namely, given an i.i.d. set
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
sampled from the probability measure
\eta
, we set

\eta_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{(x_{i},y_{i})}.

The resulting problem with joint law
\eta_{n}
is called the
empirical problem
.

Definition 68
.

If
P
is a problem, the
n
th empirical problem induced by
P
is the random problem defined by

P_{n}:=(X,Y,\eta_{n},\ell,H).

Being dependent on a random sample,
\eta_{n}
is a random measure. Consequently,
P_{n}
is a random problem. One would hope that as the size of the sample grows, the empirical problem
P_{n}
converges to the true problem
P
with probability 1. Under certain conditions, this is indeed true.

Theorem 69

(Convergence of the Empirical Problem)
.

Let
P
be a problem satisfying the following:

1.

H
is compact with respect to
d_{\ell,\eta}
.

2.

Y
is compact.

3.

\ell
is continuous.

4.

The collection
\{|\ell_{h}-\ell_{h^{\prime}}|\}_{h,h^{\prime}\in H}
forms a Glivenko-Cantelli class with respect to
\eta
.

Let
P_{n}
be the
n
th empirical problem induced by
P
. Then
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P_{n})\to 0
almost surely.

The proof, found in Appendix
A
, uses Theorem
65
to reduce to the case where
P
is a classification problem. We briefly illuminate the Glivenko-Cantelli condition in Theorem
69
by providing simpler sufficient conditions.

Proposition 70
.

Define
g_{h,h^{\prime}}(x,y):=|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell_{h^{\prime}}(x,y)|
.
Theorem
69
still holds if Condition 4 is replaced with either of the following conditions.

4a.

The collection of subgraphs
\left\{\mathrm{sg}(g_{h,h^{\prime}})\middle|h,h^{\prime}\in H\right\}
(see Definition
12
)
has finite VC dimension.

4b.

For any
h_{0},h^{\prime}_{0}\in H
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an open neighborhood
(h_{0},h^{\prime}_{0})\in U\subseteq H\times H
such that

\displaystyle\int\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in U}g_{h,h^{\prime}}(x,y)\eta(dx{\times}dy)-\varepsilon

\displaystyle<\int g_{h_{0},h^{\prime}_{0}}(x,y)\eta(dx{\times}dy)

\displaystyle<\int\inf_{(h,h^{\prime})\in U}g_{h,h^{\prime}}(x,y)\eta(dx{\times}dy)+\varepsilon.

Conditions 2 and 4a together imply condition 4, since the compactness of
Y
implies that the
g_{h,h^{\prime}}
are uniformly bounded, and hence that Proposition
13
applies. Note that Condition 4a does not depend on the joint law
\eta
. Conditions 1 and 4b together imply condition 4 as well by a theorem of
Gaenssler (1975)
.

Theorem
69
is a convergence result. A quantitative result about the
rate
of convergence would be more useful. Certainly the convergence rate will depend on properties of
P
and
P^{\prime}
; the proof of Theorem
69
suggests that the proximity of
P
and
P^{\prime}
to less complex problems is crucial.

Question 71
.

How does the rate of the convergence given by Theorem
69
depend on the properties of
P
and
P^{\prime}
? Can one bound the convergence rate for specific classes of problems?

6
The
L^{p}
-Risk Distance: Weighting Problems

In this section we introduce a variant of the Risk distance called the
L^{p}
-Risk distance
which incorporates probability measures on the predictor sets. This additional information lets us prioritize certain predictors over others by weighting them more heavily, subsequently softening the Risk distance and improving its stability. The weighting can be interpreted as a Bayesian prior, so the perspective adopted in this section is broadly aligned with the principles of Bayesian statistics. While approaches such as Bayesian decision theory
(
Berger, 1985
)
incorporate elements like loss functions, prior work does not operate on a space of problems, nor does it attempt to endow such a space with a metric structure as we do in this paper.

6.1
Weighting Predictor Sets and the
L^{p}
-Risk Distance

Many of our results thus far have required assumptions of compact predictor sets or bounded loss functions.
Without these assumptions, the Risk distance tends to be poorly-behaved.
For instance, problems such as the linear regression in Example
19
tend to be infinitely far away from problems of bounded loss under the Risk distance.
One reason for this phenomenon is the supremum in the definition of the Risk distance.
The supremum treats unreasonable predictors, such as those whose graphs are far from the support of the joint law, with the same consideration as more reasonable options.
One might reasonably complain about this design decision.

One solution to this problem is to
weight
the predictors, de-prioritizing extreme options by giving them less weight.
These weights could come from prior knowledge or belief about which predictors might perform well or from a measure of complexity of each predictor (so that more complex predictors receive lower weights). Indeed, considering these weights as part of the specification of a problem can be seen as a proxy for adding a regularization term to the constrained Bayes risk.

More formally, we provide weights on the predictor set in the form of a probability measure.
This lets us replace the supremum in the definition of
d_{\mathrm{R}}
with an
L^{p}
-type integral, effectively softening the Risk distance.

Definition 72
.

A
weighted problem
is a pair
(P,\lambda)
where

•

P
is a problem whose predictor set
H
comes equipped with a Polish topology, and

•

\lambda
is a probability measure on
H
.

When there is no chance of confusion, we may suppress the measure
\lambda
and denote the weighted problem by
P
.

Assumption 73
.

We make the following measurability assumption. Given a weighted problem
(P,\lambda)
, consider the map

\displaystyle H\times X\times Y

\displaystyle\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}

given by

\displaystyle(h,x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\ell_{h}(x,y).

Since
H
,
X
, and
Y
are topological spaces, we can equip each space with the corresponding Borel
\sigma
-algebra. We can then equip the product
H\times X\times Y
with the product
\sigma
-algebra. For any weighted problem
(P,\lambda)
, we henceforth assume that the above map
(h,x,y)\mapsto\ell_{h}(x,y)
is measurable with respect to this product
\sigma
-algebra.

Definition 74
.

Let
(P,\lambda)
and
(P^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
be weighted problems. For
p\in[1,\infty]
and
for any couplings
\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
and
\rho\in\Pi(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})
, define the
L^{p}
-risk distortion
of
\gamma
and
\rho
to be

\displaystyle\dis_{P,P^{\prime},p}(\rho,\gamma)

\displaystyle:=\left(\int\left(\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell^{\prime}(h^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\big|\,d\gamma(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\right)^{p}d\rho(h,h^{\prime})\right)^{1/p}

p\in[1,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\dis_{P,P^{\prime},\infty}(\rho,\gamma)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}[\rho]}\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell^{\prime}(h^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\big|\,d\gamma(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

p=\infty
.

The
L^{p}
-Risk distance
between weighted problems
(P,\lambda)
and
(P^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
is then defined to be

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R},p}((P,\lambda),(P^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}))

\displaystyle:=\inf_{\rho,\gamma}\dis_{P,P^{\prime},p}(\rho,\gamma)

where
\gamma
ranges over all couplings
\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
and
\rho
ranges over all couplings
\Pi(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})
.

Theorem 75
.

The
L^{p}
-Risk distance is a pseudometric on the collection of all weighted problems. Furthermore, we have

(i)

d_{\mathrm{R},p}\leq d_{\mathrm{R},q}\leq d_{\mathrm{R},\infty}
for all
1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty
;

(ii)

d_{\mathrm{R}}\leq d_{\mathrm{R},\infty}
.

Remark 76
.

Property (i) is analogous to a similar property enjoyed by the Gromov-Wasserstein distance
(
Mémoli, 2011
, Theorem 5.1 (h))
and it follows from
(
Mémoli, 2011
, Lemma 2.2)
. Property (ii) states that the
L^{\infty}
-Risk distance is never smaller than the standard Risk distance from Definition
26
. This is analogous to the fact that the Gromov-Hausdorff distance is bounded above by the
L^{\infty}
-Gromov-Wasserstein distance
(
Mémoli, 2011
, Theorem 5.1 (b))
. The proof of the triangle inequality for the
L^{p}
-Risk distance is identical to the proof of the same for the Risk distance, save for an application of Minkowski’s inequality for the outer integral. As indicated above, the proof of the other claimed properties follows similar steps as those for the Gromov-Wasserstein distance and therefore we omit them.

Example 77
.

Just as we defined the one-point problems

P_{\bullet}(c):=(\{\bullet\},\{\bullet\},\delta_{(\bullet,\bullet)},c,\{\id_{\{\bullet\}}\})

for each
c\geq 0
in Example
20
, we can define a corresponding
one-point weighted problem
by endowing
\{\id_{\{\bullet\}}\}
with the point-mass probability measure. We will still use
P_{\bullet}
to denote
P_{\bullet}(0)
. Then, in a manner similar to Example
31
, if
(P,\lambda)
is a weighted problem, one can show

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P_{\bullet})=\left(\int_{H}\left(\int_{X\times Y}\ell(h(x),y)\,\eta(dx\times dy)\right)^{p}\,\lambda(dh)\right)^{1/p}=\left(\int_{H}{\mathcal{R}}_{P}^{p}(h)\,\lambda(dh)\right)^{1/p}.

This can be seen as a kind of weighted constrained Bayes risk of
(P,\lambda)
. While the constrained Bayes risk of an unweighted problem
P
is the infimal possible risk, the above expression
d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P_{\bullet})
represents the
p
th moment of possible risks when a predictor is chosen at random according to
\lambda
. In particular,
d_{\mathrm{R},1}(P,P_{\bullet})
is simply the average risk of a predictor chosen randomly according to
\lambda
.

One can establish a connection with the usual constrained Bayes risk as follows. Assume that the problem
P
is such that its loss function
\ell
is bounded above by a constant
\ell_{\max}>0
. Then,

d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P_{\bullet}(\ell_{\max}))=\left(\int_{H}\big(\ell_{\max}-{\mathcal{R}}_{P}(h)\big)^{p}\lambda(dh)\right)^{1/p}.

If the support of
\lambda
is the whole
H
, we obtain, as
p\to\infty
, that
d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P_{\bullet}(\ell_{\max}))\to\ell_{\max}-{\mathcal{B}}(P).

The distance
d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P_{\bullet})
from Example
77
will appear again in Theorem
82
, so we establish a definition.

Definition 78
.

Given a weighted problem
P=(P,\lambda)
and
1\leq p<\infty
, the
p
-diameter of
P
is given by

d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P_{\bullet})=\left(\int_{H}\left(\int_{X\times Y}\ell(h(x),y)\,\eta(dx\times dy)\right)^{p}\,\lambda(dh)\right)^{1/p}=\left(\int_{H}{\mathcal{R}}_{P}^{p}(h)\,\lambda(dh)\right)^{1/p}.

The word “diameter” is chosen in analogy with metric geometry, where the diameter of a metric space can be found from its Gromov-Hausdorff distance from the one-point metric space.

6.2
Connection to the Gromov-Wasserstein Distance

In Section
3.5
, we saw two ways to turn metric measure spaces into problems, each of which relates the Gromov-Wasserstein distance to the Risk distance. In this section, we will draw a similar connection between the Gromov-Wasserstein distance and the
L^{p}
-Risk distance. Specifically, we will provide a way of turning metric measure spaces into weighted problems such that the
L^{1}
-Risk distance between the resulting problems aligns with a well-known modification of the Gromov-Wasserstein distance between the original spaces.

Given any metric measure space
(X,d_{X},\mu_{X})
, define a weighted problem
(P_{X},\mu_{X})
,

P_{X}:=(X,X,(\Delta_{X})_{\sharp}\mu_{X},d_{X},H_{X}),

where

•

\Delta_{X}:X\to X\times X
is the diagonal map
x\mapsto(x,x)
, and

•

H_{X}
is the set of all constant functions
X\to X
.

Here we think of
\mu_{X}
as a measure on both
X
and
H_{X}
since the two are in natural bijection.

Proposition 79
.

Let
(X,d_{X},\mu_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y},\mu_{Y})
be two metric measure spaces. Then,

d_{\mathrm{R},1}(P_{X},P_{Y})=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\Pi(\mu_{X},\mu_{Y})\\
\rho\in\Pi(\mu_{X},\mu_{Y})\end{subarray}}\int\int\big|d_{X}(x_{2},x_{1})-d_{Y}(y_{2},y_{1})\big|\,\gamma(dx_{1}{\times}dy_{1})\,\rho(dx_{2}{\times}dy_{2}).

(
\star
)

The proof of this proposition can be found in Appendix
A
.

Use
{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma,\rho)
to denote the double integral in the expression (

⋆

‣
79
). If we add the additional constraint that
\gamma=\rho
, then the expression (

⋆

‣
79
) becomes
2d_{\mathrm{GW},1}(X,Y)
. As written, (

⋆

‣
79
) is a lower bound on
2d_{\mathrm{GW},1}(X,Y)
, which could be arrived at via a “bilinear relaxation” of the optimization problem posed by
d_{\mathrm{GW},1}
. That is, one could begin with the objective function for the Gromov-Wasserstein optimization problem

{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}(\gamma):=\int\int\big|d_{X}(x_{2},x_{1})-d_{Y}(y_{2},y_{1})\big|\,\gamma(dx_{1}{\times}dy_{1})\,\gamma(dx_{2}{\times}dy_{2}),

which is quadratic in the transport plan
\gamma
, and decouple the two instances of
\gamma
to get the objective
{\mathcal{F}}(\gamma,\rho)
, which is bilinear in two transport plans. This “relaxation” was considered by
(
Mémoli, 2011
, Section 7)
for estimating the Gromov-Wasserstein distance via an alternate minimization procedure. Similar optimal transport problems, which simultaneously optimize two transport plans in a Gromov-Wasserstein-like objective function, appear in the literature. Indeed, when
X
and
Y
are finite, the expression (

⋆

‣
79
) is a special case of CO-Optimal transport
(
Vayer et al., 2020b
)
.
Sejourne et al. (2021)
apply this relaxation technique, which they call the “biconvex relaxation,” to an unbalanced variant of the Gromov-Wasserstein distance. This inspired
Beier et al. (2023)
to explore a similar relaxation for a “multi-marginal” variant of the same problem. A theoretical study of the metric properties of the right-hand side of (

⋆

‣
79
) appears in
(
Chen et al., 2022
, Appendix A.4)
.

6.3
Stability of Loss Profile Distribution

Being softer than the Risk distance, the loss profile is less stable under the
L^{p}
-Risk distance. To obtain a stability result, one cannot compare the loss profile sets directly, but instead compare certain probability distributions on the loss profile sets.

Definition 80
.

Let
(P,\lambda)
be a weighted problem. Define
F_{P}:H\to\mathbb{\prob}({\mathbb{R}})
by

F_{P}(h):=(\ell_{h})_{\sharp}\eta.

The
loss profile distribution of
P
is then defined to be
{\mathcal{L}}(P):=(F_{P})_{\sharp}\lambda
.

Note that
{\mathcal{L}}(P)\in\prob(\prob({\mathbb{R}}))
is a probability measure on the space of real probability measures. More specifically,
F_{P}(h)
is the loss profile of
h
, so
{\mathcal{L}}(P)
describes what the loss profile of a random predictor chosen according to
\lambda
is likely to be.

While Theorem
37
shows that
L(P)
is stable under the Risk distance with respect to the Hausdorff distance, the following analogous theorem shows that the measure
{\mathcal{L}}(P)
is stable under the
L^{p}
-Risk distance with respect to the Wasserstein distance.

Theorem 81
.

Let
P
and
P^{\prime}
be weighted problems. Then

d_{\mathrm{W},p}^{d_{\mathrm{W},1}}({\mathcal{L}}(P),{\mathcal{L}}(P^{\prime}))\leq d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P^{\prime})

where
d_{\mathrm{W},p}^{d_{\mathrm{W},1}}
is the
p
-Wasserstein metric on
\prob(\prob({\mathbb{R}}))
with underlying metric
d_{\mathrm{W},1}
on
\prob({\mathbb{R}})
.

The proof is in Appendix
A
.

6.4
Improved Density

A useful theorem in measure theory states that any probability measure
\mu
on a Polish space
X
is
tight
, meaning that for any
\varepsilon>0
, there is a compact subset
K\subseteq X
with
\mu(K)>1-\varepsilon
. In a slogan, “Polish probability spaces are almost compact.” Applying this principle to our setting suggests that any weighted problem should be close to one with a compact predictor set. This can be made rigorous with a density theorem.

Theorem 82
.

Let
p\geq 1
and let
(P,\lambda)
be a weighted problem of finite
p
-diameter. Then there exists a compact weighted problem
(P^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
with
d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P^{\prime})<\varepsilon
.

Additionally, a result analogous to Theorem
65
still holds for the
L^{p}
-Risk distance.

Proposition 83
.

Let
p\geq 1
and let
(P,\lambda)
be a compact weighted problem. Then there exists a weighted classification problem
(P^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
with
d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P^{\prime})<\varepsilon
.

The proofs of both Theorem
82
and Proposition
83
can be found in Appendix
A
. Chaining these two density results together proves the following corollary.

Corollary 84
.

Under
d_{\mathrm{R},p}
for
p\geq 1
, weighted classification problems are dense in the space of weighted problems of finite
p
-diameter.

Example 85
.

Consider the linear regression problem
P
from Example
19
. If we endow the predictor set of
P
with a joint law satisfying some finite moment assumptions, the resulting weighted problem has finite
p
-diameter.
Corollary
84
then tells us that
P
can be approximated arbitrarily well by weighted classification problems under
d_{\mathrm{R},p}
.

More precisely, let
P
be a linear regression problem, and suppose the predictor set

H=\left\{x\mapsto a^{T}x+b\,\middle|\,(a,b)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}\right\}

is equipped with a probability measure
\lambda
. We can think of both
\lambda
and
\eta
as probability measures on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}
. If

1.

all
n+1
marginals of
\eta
have finite second moment, and

2.

all
n+1
marginals of
\lambda
have finite
2p
-th moment,

then
\diam_{p}(P)<\infty
.

To prove this, we can write

\displaystyle\big(\operatorname{diam}_{p}(P)\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left(\int\left(\int\ell(a^{T}x+b,y)\,\eta(dx\times dy)\right)^{p}\,\lambda(da\times db)\right)^{1/2p}

\displaystyle=\left(\int\left(\left(\int(a^{T}x+b-y)^{2}\,\eta(dx\times dy)\right)^{1/2}\right)^{2p}\,\lambda(da\times db)\right)^{1/2p}.

By liberally applying Minkowski’s inequality and simplifying, we can bound this expression by the sum

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\int a_{i}^{2p}\,\lambda(da\times db)\right)^{1/2p}\left(\int x_{i}^{2}\,\eta(dx\times dy)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(\int\left(b\right)^{2p}\,\lambda(da\times db)\right)^{1/2p}+\left(\int y^{2}\,\eta(dx\times dy)\right)^{1/2}.

These integrals are the moments of
\eta
and
\lambda
that we have assumed to be finite.

6.5
Improved Stability

The
L^{p}
-Risk distance enjoys many of the same stability and density results as the Risk distance. Indeed, we will soon see that many of these results can be
strengthened
for the
L^{p}
-Risk distance. That is, the
L^{p}
-Risk distance enjoys
stronger
stability and density properties than the Risk distance.

First we will establish that the
L^{p}
-Risk distance inherits some stability results from the Risk distance, saving the improvements for later.
Many of our bounds on the Risk distance are proven using the bound

d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})\leq\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R_{\mathrm{diag}},\gamma)

(2)

where
R_{\mathrm{diag}}
is the diagonal correspondence, which is valid whenever
P
and
P^{\prime}
share a predictor set.
Hence we can extend the results of those sections using the following lemma.

Lemma 86
.

Let
(P,\lambda)
and
(P^{\prime},\lambda)
be weighted problems that share a predictor set
H
and predictor weights
\lambda
. Then

d_{\mathrm{R},p}((P,\lambda),(P^{\prime},\lambda))\leq\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R_{\mathrm{diag}},\gamma)

where
R_{\mathrm{diag}}
is the diagonal correspondence of
H
with itself.

Proof.

First write

d_{\mathrm{R},p}((P,\lambda),(P^{\prime},\lambda))\leq\dis_{P,P^{\prime},p}(\rho_{\mathrm{diag}},\gamma)

where
\rho_{\mathrm{diag}}
is the diagonal coupling of
\lambda
with itself.

Next we bound the
L^{p}
-risk distortion by the
L^{\infty}
-risk distortion as follows:

\displaystyle\dis_{P,P^{\prime},p}(\rho_{\mathrm{diag}},\gamma)

\displaystyle\leq\dis_{P,P^{\prime},\infty}(\rho_{\mathrm{diag}},\gamma)

\displaystyle=\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}{[\rho_{\mathrm{diag}}}]}\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell^{\prime}(h^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\big|\,d\gamma(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R_{\mathrm{diag}}}\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell^{\prime}(h^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\big|\,d\gamma(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R_{\mathrm{diag}},\gamma),

where the second inequality follows from the fact that the support of
\rho_{\mathrm{diag}}
lies within the diagonal
R_{\mathrm{diag}}\subseteq H\times H
.
∎

Condition 2 from Theorem
28
, the Risk distance bounds of Sections
4.3
(Stability under bias),
4.4
(Stability under noise), and
5.4
(Convergence of empirical problems) are all proven via the diagonal bound (
2
).
6
6

6

In addition to the diagonal bound, the proof of Theorem
69
also uses the density of classification problems (Theorem
67
). However, the proof of the analogous result for weighted problems is still valid if we replace this step with an application of the density of weighted classification problems (Theorem
83
).

By Lemma
86
, these results still hold if we replace the Risk distance with the
L^{p}
-Risk distance and replace both problems with weighted problems that share a common weight measure
\lambda
.
That is, in the above results, we can replace each instance of
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})
with
d_{\mathrm{R},p}((P,\lambda),(P^{\prime},\lambda))
and they will still hold true.

Moreover, some of our results for the Risk distance can be strengthened for the
L^{p}
-Risk distance. Indeed, we have already seen that the density results of Section
5.3
can be strengthened into Theorem
82
. We can strengthen some stability results in the same manner. For instance, the
L^{p}
-Risk distance satisfies the following version of Condition 2 in which the supremum is replaced by an integral.

Proposition 87
.

Let
(P,\lambda)
and
(P^{\prime},\lambda)
be weighted problems with

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=(X,Y,\eta,\ell,H)

\displaystyle P^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(X,Y,\eta,\ell^{\prime},H)

Then

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P^{\prime})\leq\left(\int\left(\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell^{\prime}(h(x),y)\big|\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)\,\right)^{p}\lambda(dh)\right)^{1/p}.

Proof.

Bound the
L^{p}
-Risk distance by selecting the diagonal couplings.
∎

We can also modify Theorem
51
by replacing the Hausdorff distance with a Wasserstein distance.

Proposition 88
.

Let
(P,\lambda)
and
(P,\lambda^{\prime})
be weighted problems with

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle:=(X,Y,\eta,\ell,H)

\displaystyle P^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=(X,Y,\eta,\ell,H^{\prime}).

Let
i:H\to H\cup H^{\prime}
and
j:H^{\prime}\to H\cup H^{\prime}
be the inclusion maps, and endow
H\cup H^{\prime}
with the pseudometric
d_{\ell,\eta}(h_{1},h_{2}):=\|\ell_{h_{1}}-\ell_{h_{2}}\|_{L^{1}(\eta)}
introduced in Section
5.2
.

Then

d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P^{\prime})\leq d_{\mathrm{W},p}^{d_{\ell,\eta}}(i_{\sharp}\lambda,j_{\sharp}\lambda^{\prime}).

Proof.

Selecting the diagonal coupling between
\eta
and itself gives us the bound

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\rho\in\Pi(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})}\left(\int\left(\int\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell_{h^{\prime}}(x,y)\big|\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)\right)^{p}\rho(dh{\times}dh^{\prime})\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\rho\in\Pi(i_{\sharp}\lambda,j_{\sharp}\lambda^{\prime})}\left(\int\left(\int\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell_{h^{\prime}}(x,y)\big|\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)\right)^{p}\rho(dh{\times}dh^{\prime})\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\rho\in\Pi(i_{\sharp}\lambda,j_{\sharp}\lambda^{\prime})}\left(\int\left(d_{\ell,\eta}(h,h^{\prime})\right)^{p}\rho(dh{\times}dh^{\prime})\right)^{1/p}=d_{\mathrm{W},p}^{d_{\ell,\eta}}(i_{\sharp}\lambda,j_{\sharp}\lambda^{\prime})

as desired.
∎

Proposition
44
