can be modified for weighted problems as well. We first construct an analog of the metric
s_{\ell,H}
from Section
4.3
. Replacing the supremum over
H
in the definition of
s_{\ell,H}
with an
L^{p}
-style integral against
\lambda
, we arrive at the definition

\displaystyle s_{\ell,\lambda,p}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})):=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{H}\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell(h(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\big|^{p}\,\lambda(dh)\right)^{1/p}.

In other words, to determine the distance between
(x,y)
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
, we ask how different the loss on the observation
(x,y)
will be from the loss on
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
for an average predictor.
We can now state our stability result.

Proposition 89
.

Let
(P,\lambda)
be a weighted problem, and let
(P^{\prime},\lambda)
be a weighted problem that is identical to
P
except possibly for its joint law
\eta^{\prime}
. Then

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P^{\prime})\leq d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{s_{\ell,\lambda,p}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime}).

Proof.

By selecting the diagonal coupling between
\lambda
and itself and applying Minkowski’s inequality for integrals, we can bound
d_{\mathrm{R},p}
by

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}\left(\int\left(\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell(h(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\big|\,\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})\right)^{p}\lambda(dh)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}\int\left(\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell(h(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\big|^{p}\,\lambda(dh)\right)^{1/p}\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})

\displaystyle=d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{s_{\ell,\lambda,p}}(\eta,\eta^{\prime}),

giving us the desired bound.
∎

Lastly, we turn our attention to stability under noise.
We have shown in Section
4.4
that the Risk distance is stable with respect to noise in the joint law when
\ell
is a metric.
By contrast, for a general
\ell
, the Risk distance
d_{\mathrm{R}}
can be highly sensitive to changes in the joint law. The following example demonstrates this sensitivity.

Example 90
.

Define a problem

P=(\{\bullet\},\mathbb{R},\delta_{0},\ell,H)

where
\ell(y,y^{\prime})=(y-y^{\prime})^{2}
, and
H
is the collection of all functions
\{\bullet\}\to\mathbb{R}
, making
H
itself homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
.
7
7

7

In other words, we can identify
H
with
\mathbb{R}
.
Now, for any
\varepsilon>0
, define

P_{\varepsilon}=\left(\{\bullet\},\mathbb{R},\frac{1}{2}(\delta_{0}+\delta_{\varepsilon}),\ell,H\right).

We can think of
P_{\varepsilon}
as
P
with some noise; when making an observation, there is a 50% of observing the correct
y
-value of 0, and a 50% chance of making a small error and reading a
y
-value of
\varepsilon
. We would hope that
P_{\varepsilon}
would converge to
P
under
d_{\mathrm{R}}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, but this is not true. There is only one coupling between
\delta_{0}
and
1/2(\delta_{0}+\delta_{\varepsilon})
, so the Risk distance is

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\inf_{R\in{\mathcal{C}}(H,H)}\sup_{(a,b)\in R}\int\int\big|(a-y)^{2}-(b-y^{\prime})^{2}|\,\delta_{0}(dy)\left(\frac{1}{2}\delta_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\varepsilon}\right)(dy^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\inf_{R\in{\mathcal{C}}(H,H)}\sup_{(a,b)\in R}\left[\frac{1}{2}\big|a^{2}-b^{2}\big|+\frac{1}{2}\big|a^{2}-(b-\varepsilon)^{2}\big|\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\inf_{R\in{\mathcal{C}}(H,H)}\sup_{(a,b)\in R}\big|b^{2}-(b-\varepsilon)^{2}\big|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sup_{b\in\mathbb{R}}|2b\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{2}|=\infty.

The metric
d_{\mathrm{R},p}
enjoys a stronger stability than
d_{\mathrm{R}}
with respect to noise in a problem’s joint law.
We use a more general noise model than the one outlined in Section
4.4
, allowing for noise in the
X
direction as well as the
Y
direction. More specifically, we let our noise be given by any Markov kernel
N:X\times Y\to\prob(X\times Y)
. That is, given a problem
P
, define
P_{N}
to be the same problem but with joint law on
X\times Y
given by

\displaystyle\eta\cdot N(A):=\int_{X\times Y}N(x,y)(A)\,\eta(dx\times dy)

for all measurable
A\subseteq X\times Y
. We justify the use of Markov kernel composition notation by thinking of the measure
\eta
as a Markov kernel
\eta:\{\bullet\}\to\prob(X\times Y)
.

We can now state our improved result about stability under noise.

Theorem 91
.

Let
(P,\lambda)
be a weighted problem, and
N:X\times Y\to\prob(X\times Y)
a Markov kernel. Then

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P_{N})\leq d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{s_{\ell,\lambda,p}}(\delta_{\id_{X\times Y}},N).

Here
\delta_{\id_{X\times Y}}
is the Markov kernel sending
(x,y)
to
\delta_{(x,y)}
, and
d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{s_{\ell,\lambda,p}}
is the Wasserstein metric on Markov kernels
X\times Y\to\prob(X\times Y)
when
X\times Y
is equipped with the joint law
\eta
and the metric
s_{\ell,\lambda,p}
.

The proof can be found in Appendix
A
.

Example 92
.

Let
(P,\lambda)
be a weighted problem with
P=(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R},\eta,\ell,H)
, where

•

\ell(y,y^{\prime}):=(y-y^{\prime})^{2}
,

•

H:=\{f_{a}|a\in\mathbb{R}\}
, where
f_{a}(x):=ax
,

•

\lambda
is the standard normal distribution.

Let
N:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\prob(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
be a noise kernel that applies
x
-dependent noise in the vertical direction. That is, if
i_{x}^{y}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the function
i_{x}^{y}(y^{\prime})=(x,y+y^{\prime})
, set

N(x,y)=(i_{x}^{y})_{\sharp}M(x)

for some Markov kernel
M:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. Assume that for all
x
,
M(x)
is a symmetric measure with finite 2nd moment
v(x)
and 4th moment
k(x)
.
Theorem
91
gives us
d_{R,2}(P,P_{N})\leq d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{s_{\ell,\lambda,2}}(\delta_{\id_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}},N).
The right hand side is difficult to exactly compute, so we will compute the larger quantity
d_{\mathrm{W},2}^{s_{\ell,\lambda,2}}(\delta_{\id_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}},N)
.

We first produce an explicit formula for
s_{\ell,\lambda,2}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))
.

\displaystyle s_{\ell,\lambda,2}^{2}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\int\left(\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell_{h}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\right)^{2}\,\lambda(dh)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left((ax-y)^{2}-(ax^{\prime}-y^{\prime})^{2}\right)^{2}\frac{e^{-a^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}da.

After expanding the polynomial term, the integral can be split into five terms that represent various moments of the standard normal distribution. We arrive at the polynomial

s_{\ell,\lambda,2}^{2}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))=3(x^{2}-x^{\prime 2})^{2}+4(xy-x^{\prime}y^{\prime})^{2}+(y^{2}-y^{\prime 2})^{2}+2(x^{2}-x^{\prime 2})(y^{2}-y^{\prime 2}).

Next we can compute
d_{W,2}^{s_{\ell,\lambda,2}}(\delta_{(x,y)},N(x,y))
for any fixed
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Indeed, there is only one coupling between
\delta_{(x,y)}
and
N(x,y)
, so we can write

\displaystyle\left(d_{W,2}^{s_{\ell,\lambda,2}}\right)^{2}(\delta_{(x,y)},N(x,y))

\displaystyle=\int s_{\ell,\lambda,2}^{2}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))\,N(x,y)(dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int s_{\ell,\lambda,2}^{2}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))\,(i_{x}^{y})_{\sharp}M(x)(dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int s_{\ell,\lambda,2}^{2}((x,y),(x,y^{\prime}+y))\,M(x)(dy^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int 4x^{2}y^{\prime 2}+y^{\prime 2}(y^{\prime}+2y)^{2}\,M(x)(dy^{\prime}).

Again, expanding the polynomial terms and splitting the integral reduces the problem to moment computations. Recalling that
M(x)
is symmetric and hence has vanishing odd moments, we arrive at the expression

\left(d_{W,2}^{s_{\ell,\lambda,2}}\right)^{2}(\delta_{(x,y)},N(x,y))=4v(x)x^{2}+4v(x)y^{2}+k(x).

Integrating against
\eta
gives us an expression for
\left(d_{\mathrm{W},2}^{s_{\ell,\lambda,2}}\right)^{2}(\delta_{\id_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}},N)
, and hence an upper bound for
d_{\mathrm{R},p}^{2}(P,P_{N})
.

d_{\mathrm{R},p}^{2}(P,P_{N})\leq\int\left[4v(x)\left(\int y^{2}\,\beta(x)(dy)\right)+4x^{2}v(x)+k(x)\right]\,\alpha(dx)

where
\alpha
is the first marginal of
\eta
, and
\beta
is the disintegration. We take a moment to interpret the integrand of the outer integral. The integral
\int y^{2}\beta(x)(dy)
is the second moment of
\beta(x)
. Hence the term
v(x)\int y^{2}\beta(x)(dy)
measures the correlation between the spread of
\beta(x)
and the noise
\sigma^{2}(x)
applied at
x
. The term
x^{2}\sigma^{2}(x)
measures the application of noise far from the origin. Hence this bound suggests that noise has an effect on the squared Risk distance proportional to its 4th moment, with an additional effect proportional to its 2nd moment if applied at
x
-values far from zero, or where
\beta(x)
is already spread out.

7
The Connected Risk Distance: Risk Landscapes

Having discussed the weighted variant of the Risk distance, we now introduce and study one more variant, called the
Connected Risk distance
. This variant is motivated by the observation that the Risk distance is insensitive to the contours of a problem’s risk landscape. We will show that by strengthening the Risk distance into the Connected Risk distance, a topological descriptor of the risk landscape called the
Reeb graph
can be made to exhibit stability under the Connected Risk distance without breaking many of the stability results that the Risk distance enjoys. The Reeb graph of the risk landscape of a problem
P
encodes information about the inherent
complexity
associated to solving
P
.

7.1
Insensitivities of the Risk distance

We saw in section
4.1
that the constrained Bayes risk of a problem is stable under the Risk distance. The constrained Bayes risk
{\mathcal{B}}(P)
of a problem
P
is a sufficiently descriptive invariant if we plan to solve
P
by exhaustively searching the predictor set
H
for a predictor with minimal risk. If we instead wish to use a more practical search method like gradient descent, then
{\mathcal{B}}(P)
is a woefully incomplete descriptor. The specific structure of the risk function
{\mathcal{R}}_{P}:H\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
becomes crucially important, rather than just its infimum. Is
{\mathcal{R}}_{P}
rife with local minima? What are the risks of the local minima? What are their basins like? The Risk distance is not sensitive to these kinds of concerns.

Example 93
.

Define a problem

P:=([0,1],\{0,1\},\eta,\ell,H)

and a family of problems

P_{t}:=([0,1],\{0,1\},\eta,\ell,H_{t}),t\in(0,1],

where

1.

\ell
is the 0-1 loss
\ell(y,y^{\prime}):=1_{y\neq y^{\prime}}
.

2.

\eta
is the uniform distribution on
[0,1]\times\{0\}
.

3.

H:=\left\{1_{[0,a)}\middle|a\in[0,1]\right\}\cup\left\{1_{(a,1]}\middle|a\in[0,1]\right\}
.

4.

H_{t}:=\left\{1_{[0,a)}\middle|a\in[t,1]\right\}\cup\left\{1_{(a,1]}\middle|a\in[0,1]\right\}
.

Here we take the convention that
[0,0)=(1,1]=\emptyset
.
We claim that
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P_{t})\leq t
. Indeed, by Theorem
51
,

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P_{t})

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathrm{H}}^{L^{1}(\eta)}\left(\left\{\ell_{h}\middle|h\in H\right\},\left\{\ell_{h^{\prime}}\middle|h^{\prime}\in H_{t}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\sup_{h\in H}\inf_{h^{\prime}\in H_{t}}\|\ell_{h}-\ell_{h^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}(\eta)}

\displaystyle=\sup_{h\in H\setminus H_{t}}\inf_{h^{\prime}\in H_{t}}\int_{0}^{1}\big|\ell(h(x),0)-\ell(h^{\prime}(x),0)\big|\,dx

\displaystyle=\sup_{a\in[0,t)}\inf_{h^{\prime}\in H_{t}}\int_{0}^{1}\big|1_{1_{[0,a)}(x)\neq 0}-1_{h^{\prime}(x)\neq 0}\big|\,dx

\displaystyle=\sup_{a\in[0,t)}\inf_{h^{\prime}\in H_{t}}\int_{0}^{1}\big|1_{[0,a)}(x)-h^{\prime}(x)\big|\,dx.

Choosing
h^{\prime}=0
gives us the upper bound bound

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{a\in[0,t)}\int_{0}^{1}1_{[0,a)}(x)\,dx=t.

Hence
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P_{t},P)\to 0
as
t\to 0
.

{\mathcal{R}}_{P}
0
1
H
{\mathcal{R}}_{P_{t}}
0
1
t
H_{t}

Figure 6
:
The risk landscapes for
P
and
P_{t}
in Example
93
.

At the same time, the risk landscapes of
P
and
P_{t}
look very different for any
t>0
. These risk landscapes are depicted in Figure
6
. Under the metric
d_{\ell,\eta}
,
H
is topologically a circle. The risk function
{\mathcal{R}}_{P}:H\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
has one local minimum, which is achieved at the function
h(x)\equiv 0
. For
t>0
,
H_{t}
is formed from
H
by removing a small segment of the circle, making
H_{t}
topologically a closed interval under
d_{\ell,\eta}
. Then
{\mathcal{R}}_{P_{t}}
has a local minimum at each end of the endpoints of the interval, one at
h(x)=0
and the other at
h(x)=1_{[0,t)}
.

In this section, we strengthen the Risk distance to exert more control over the risk landscape. Inspired by
Bauer et al. (2021)
, we restrict the correspondences in the definition of
d_{\mathrm{R}}
to only those satisfying a certain connectivity property.

7.2
The Connected Risk distance

If
H,H^{\prime}
are topological spaces, define
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{con}}(H,H^{\prime})
to be the set of correspondences
R\subseteq H\times H^{\prime}
such that the projections
R\to H
and
R\to H^{\prime}
are
inverse connected
, meaning the preimage of any connected set is connected.

Definition 94

(Connected Risk distance)
.

\displaystyle d_{\operatorname{RC}}(P,P^{\prime}):=

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\\
R\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{con}}(H,H^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R,\gamma)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\\
R\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{con}}(H,H^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\int\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big|\,\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime}).

Note that the definition of
d_{\operatorname{RC}}
is the same as that of
d_{\mathrm{R}}
(Definition
26
), except that the correspondence
R
is chosen from
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{con}}(H,H^{\prime})
.
We claim
d_{\operatorname{RC}}
is a pseudometric. The only nontrivial property is the triangle inequality, which requires a modified version of the gluing lemma.

Lemma 95
.

Let
H_{1},H_{2},H_{3}
be topological spaces, and let
R_{1,2}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{con}}(H_{1},H_{2})
,
R_{2,3}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{con}}(H_{2},H_{3})
. Then there exists an
R\subseteq H_{1}\times H_{2}\times H_{3}
such that the projection maps onto each of the three coordinates are all inverse connected, and such that
\operatorname{proj_{1,2}}(R)=R_{1,2}
and
\operatorname{proj_{2,3}}(R)=R_{2,3}
.

With this lemma in hand, we can prove the triangle inequality in exactly the same way that we did for
d_{\mathrm{R}}
.
The proof of Lemma
95
can be found in Appendix
A
.

Remark 96
.

Note that
d_{\operatorname{RC}}
is a larger metric than
d_{\mathrm{R}}
, since
R
is infimized over
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{con}}(H_{1},H_{2})\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}(H_{1},H_{2})
. Therefore we would expect
d_{\operatorname{RC}}
to be less stable than
d_{\mathrm{R}}
. Some of the stability results of
d_{\mathrm{R}}
were proven by selecting
R
to be the diagonal coupling between a predictor set
H
and itself. Since
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{con}}(H,H)
still contains the diagonal coupling, these proofs apply just as well to
d_{\operatorname{RC}}
, just as we saw with the
L^{p}
-Risk distance in Section
6.5
. Specifically, Condition 2 from Section
3.3
, as well as the results of Sections
4.3
(stability under bias) and
4.4
(stability under noise) still apply to the Connected Risk distance.

Additionally, since
d_{\operatorname{RC}}
is larger than
d_{\mathrm{R}}
, the problem descriptors stable under the former are also stable under the latter. In particular, the results of Section
4.1
and Section
4.2

still hold for
d_{\operatorname{RC}}
; still stable under the Connected Risk distance are the loss profile set and consequently the Bayes risk, as well as the Rademacher complexity for an arbitrarily large sample size.

7.3
Risk Landscapes and Reeb Graphs

We will show that a certain topological summary of the risk landscape of a problem, known as the
Reeb graph
, is stable under the Connected Risk distance.

Definition 97

(Induced Reeb Graph)
.

Let
X
be a topological space and
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
a continuous function. Define an equivalence relation on
X
by declaring that
x\sim y
if and only if
x
and
y
belong to the same connected component of some level set of
f
. Define
F:=X/{\sim}
. Let
q:F\to\mathbb{R}
be the unique continuous function that factors through
f
. The pair
(F,q)
is called the
Reeb graph induced by
f
.

Figure 7:
An illustration of a function
f
on the unit square and its induced Reeb graph. The two local minima of the function produce downward-pointing leaves of equal height on the Reeb graph. Similarly, the three local maxima produce upward-pointing leaves, with the lower maximum producing a lower leaf.

Typically, we picture a Reeb graph
(F,q)
by thinking of the function
q
as a “height” function; we imagine that
F
is situated in space so that
q(x)
is the altitude of the point
x
. See Figure
7
. In the computer science literature, many authors define the Reeb graph by declaring that
x\sim y
if and only if
x
and
y
lie in the same
path-connected
component instead of the same
connected
component. While these definitions are not equivalent in general, they are equivalent under reasonable topological assumptions, such as when the level sets of
f
are locally path-connected.

Reeb graphs are used as summaries of landscapes induced by real functions. While originally designed by Georges Reeb to study functions on smooth manifolds
(
Reeb, 1946
)
and independently rediscovered by
Kronrod (1950)
, Reeb graphs were first popularized as a computational tool by
Shinagawa et al. (1991)
. Since then, Reeb graphs have grown in popularity, finding applications in the comparison
(
Hilaga et al., 2001
;
Escolano et al., 2013
)
, parameterization
(
Patanè et al., 2004
;
Zhang et al., 2005
)
, and denoising
(
Wood et al., 2004
)
of shapes, in symmetry detection
(
Thomas and Natarajan, 2011
)
, and in sketching of static
(
Chazal and Sun, 2013
;
Ge et al., 2011
)
and time-varying
(
Edelsbrunner et al., 2004
)
data, among many others. See
Biasotti et al. (2008)
;
Yan et al. (2021)
for surveys.

While it is easiest to imagine “Reeb graphs” as
topological graphs
8
8

8

The following definition will suffice: A
topological graph
is a topological space produced from a graph by realizing the vertices as isolated points and the edges as arcs connecting them; see
(
Hatcher, 2005
, Chapter 0)
for details.
with attached height functions, one should be wary that, in general, the Reeb graph of a function need not admit the structure of a topological graph. Thankfully, many classes of spaces and functions are known to induce Reeb graphs which are also topological graphs. One list of sufficient conditions is provided by
de Silva et al. (2016, Example 2.2)
.

{\mathcal{R}}_{P}
{\mathcal{B}}(P)
\reeb(P)

Figure 8:
The Reeb graph of some problem
P
. Based on the downward-pointing leaves of the Reeb graph, this problem’s risk landscape seems to have three basins. The height of the lowest point in the Reeb graph corresponds to the constrained Bayes risk
{\mathcal{B}}(P)
.

Definition 98
.

Let
P
be a problem. The
Reeb graph of
P
, denoted
\reeb(P)
, is the Reeb graph induced by the risk function
{\mathcal{R}}_{P}:H\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

The Reeb graph of a problem
P
is a summary of the risk landscape. (See Figure
8
.) For instance, the height of the lowest point of
\reeb(P)
is the constrained Bayes risk. If
{\mathcal{R}}_{P}
has a unique minimizer, then
\reeb(P)
will have a unique lowest point. Conversely, if
\reeb(P)
has a unique lowest point, then the minimizers of
{\mathcal{R}}_{P}
form a connected set. Local minima appear as downward-pointing leaves, with the height of the edges corresponding to the depth of the basins around those minima. Similarly, local maxima are represented by upward-pointing leaves. The Reeb graph of
P
therefore captures information that can be useful for quantifying the complexity of the problem
P
.

Reeb graphs can be compared via any of several related metrics. The largest such metric in popular use is called the
universal distance

(
Bauer et al., 2021
)
.

Definition 99

(Universal Distance on Reeb graphs)
.

The
universal distance
between two Reeb graphs
(F,f)
and
(G,g)
is given by

d_{\mathrm{U}}((F,f),(G,g)):=\inf_{Z,\widehat{f},\widehat{g}:Z\to\mathbb{R}}\|\widehat{f}-\widehat{g}\|_{\infty}

where the infimum ranges over all spaces
Z
and functions
\widehat{f},\widehat{g}:Z\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\widehat{f}
induces the Reeb graph
(F,f)
and
\widehat{g}
induces the Reeb graph
(G,g)
.

Even with respect to the universal distance, the Reeb graph of a problem is stable under the Connected Risk distance.

Theorem 100
.

Let
P
and
P^{\prime}
be problems. Then

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{B}}(P)-{\mathcal{B}}(P^{\prime})|\leq d_{\mathrm{U}}(\reeb(P),\reeb(P^{\prime}))\leq d_{\operatorname{RC}}(P,P^{\prime}).

Hence the Reeb graph of a problem is a stronger descriptor than the constrained Bayes risk while remaining stable under the Connected Risk distance. The proof can be found in Appendix
A
.

Example 101
.

Let
P
be a problem whose loss
\ell
is a metric, such as a 0-1 loss in a classification problem, mean absolute error in a regression problem, or a Wasserstein distance in a probability estimation problem. Suppose that we expect noise in the labels of our data such that the average distance under
\ell
between the true label and the observed label is
\varepsilon_{1}
. Write
\eta^{\prime}
for the noisy distribution from which the observed data is drawn. Suppose also that the loss function
\ell
is difficult to exactly compute or has poor optimization properties, but there is an approximation
\ell^{\prime}
that does not share the same issues and is
\varepsilon_{2}
-close to
\ell
in the supremum norm. Replacing
\eta
with the noisy
\eta^{\prime}
and
\ell
with the approximation
\ell^{\prime}
could introduce new local minima to the risk function. We would like to understand how bad these local minima can be. Let
P^{\prime}=(X,Y,\eta^{\prime},\ell,H)
and
P^{\prime\prime}=(X,Y,\eta^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},H)
. We aim to bound the universal distance between the Reeb graphs of the idealized problem
P
and the corrupted problem
P^{\prime\prime}
. Since Theorem
49
applies to the Connected Risk distance (Remark
96
), we have

d_{\operatorname{RC}}(P,P^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon_{1}.

Since
d_{\operatorname{RC}}
also satisfies Condition 2 from Section
3.3
, we also have

d_{\operatorname{RC}}(P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime})\leq\max_{h\in H}\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell^{\prime}(h(x),y)\big|\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)\leq\varepsilon_{2}.

Then, using the triangle inequality for
d_{\operatorname{RC}}
and Theorem
100
, we can write the bound

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{U}}(\reeb(P),\reeb(P^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq d_{\operatorname{RC}}(P,P^{\prime\prime})\leq d_{\operatorname{RC}}(P,P^{\prime})+d_{\operatorname{RC}}(P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime})\leq\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}.

In other words, if we replace the idealized problem
P
with the corrupted problem
P^{\prime\prime}
, the Reeb graph can change by at most
\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}
in the universal distance. Hence any downward-pointing leaf of
\reeb(P)
of depth more than
2(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2})
from top to bottom has a corresponding downward-pointing leaf in
\reeb(P^{\prime\prime})
, and the difference in the heights of the tips of two corresponding leaves is at most
\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}
. We conclude that a basin of depth greater than
2(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2})
in the risk landscape of the original problem corresponds to some basin in the corrupted problem, and the risk at the bottom of two corresponding basins differ by at most
\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}
. Similarly, any new downward-pointing leaves in the Reeb graph created by corrupting
P
to get
P^{\prime\prime}
can be of length at most
2(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2})
from top to bottom, so any new basins introduced by the corruption can be of depth at most
2(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2})
from the deepest to highest point of the basin.

8
Discussion

Machine learning is currently engaged in a rapid and wide-ranging exploration of its problem space. This paper proposes a guiding and organizing principle, focusing on supervised learning. As the field grows explosively, such structure is increasingly needed to make sense of emerging approaches. In this work, we argue that, given a notion of distance between supervised learning problems, various corruptions and modifications of problems can be seen from a geometric perspective. We propose the Risk distance and two variants thereof as reasonable choices for this distance notion, bolstering our claims with a garniture of stability results.
The development in this paper can be seen as a direct descendent of earlier work in the machine
learning community on reductions between learning problems, surrogate
losses and different forms of regularization.

The present work has opened the way for further understanding of machine
learning analogous to several productive, related research programs. The idea of the
function
space
lies at the center of the abstract theory of functional analysis
(
Birkhoff and Kreyszig, 1984
, page 259)
, the development of which was highly productive for
20th century mathematics
(
Dieudonné, 1981
)
. Similarly productive for metric geometry was the work of
Gromov (1981)
, which created the metric space of all metric spaces.
Other similar examples include category theory, which is concerned with putting relational structures on collection of objects
(
Mac Lane, 2013
)
, and the theories of optimal transport
(
Villani, 2008
)
and information geometry
(
Amari, 2016
)
, which both propose geometric spaces of probability measures.

Common among all of these examples is the viewpoint that
to understand an object, it is productive to place it in a larger space of similar objects
.
Following this viewpoint, the present work opens the door to similar techniques by establishing candidates for
spaces of supervised learning problems
.
In particular, the definition of the Risk distance directly echoes the motifs of an established lineage of distances from metric geometry and optimal transport (Section
2.3
).
We have shown that the geometry is meaningful; the geometric notion of a geodesic is related to optimal couplings and correspondences (Section
5.1
), and that the topological density of classification problems (Section
5.3
) is useful in proving the convergence of empirical problems (Section
5.4
).
Throughout Section
6
, the weighting
\lambda
placed on the set of predictors can be interpreted as a Bayesian prior—an approach aligned with the principles of Bayesian statistics. However, classical Bayesian frameworks do not consider a space of problems or attempt to endow it with a metric structure, as we do here. While Bayesian decision theory
(
Berger, 1985
)
incorporates elements such as loss functions, it does not address the geometry of the problem space. It is conceivable that the present work may offer new perspectives in that direction.

More generally, we are optimistic that further geometric, topological, and even categorical studies of the space of problems can shed light on questions of interest in supervised learning. For example, the following concrete research directions appear to be natural candidates for exploration through the lens of the ideas developed in this paper:

•

Convergence Rates for the Empirical Problem
. We posed Question
71
aimed at making Theorem
69
, on the convergence of the empirical problem, more explicit and quantitative.

•

Leverage the framework to design broader, practical reductions between learning problems
, allowing more components of ML tasks to vary. As shown in Section
5.3
, classification problems are dense—in the sense of the Risk distance—within the space of all compact problems. This suggests a structural connection between classification and regression problems, despite their apparent differences. Conversely, it is natural to investigate reductions in the opposite direction: for example, reducing a classification problem with severe label noise to a regression problem with biased sampling, where both problems are close in Risk distance. Such a reduction would enable robust regression techniques to be repurposed for noisy classification tasks, facilitating the transfer of insights across distinct problem classes.

•

Analyze the robustness of other theoretical results—e.g., fast rates of convergence—through the lens of the Risk distance (or suitable variants thereof)
. In particular, is “fast convergence” a stable property under appropriate structural assumptions on the problems?

•

Assess the empirical value of the optimal couplings and correspondences produced by our theory.
An optimal coupling and correspondence (as studied in Section
5.2
) between two problems provide a kind of dictionary relating the two problems. What new insights can be drawn from such a dictionary?

•

Investigate concrete models of
sampling bias
within our framework
, such as distribution shift
(
Quiñonero-Candela et al., 2009
)
and selection bias
(
Meng, 2018
)
, which arise in many practical learning scenarios.

comments on a draft.

Appendix A
Relegated Proofs

A.1
Proofs and Details from Section
3

Theorem
27
.

The only nontrivial pseudometric axiom to prove is the triangle inequality. Furthermore, the claim that
d_{\mathrm{R}}
vanishes on weakly isomorphic problems will follow from the triangle inequality and the fact that
d_{\mathrm{R}}
satisfies Condition 1 (Theorem
28
). That is, if
P^{\prime\prime}
is common simulation of
P
and
P^{\prime}
, then
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})\leq d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime\prime})+d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime\prime},P^{\prime})=0+0
. Hence we need only prove that
d_{\mathrm{R}}
satisfies the triangle inequality.

Let
P_{i}=(X_{i},Y_{i},\eta_{i},\ell_{i},H_{i})
for
i=1,2,3
.
Let
\gamma_{1,2}\in\Pi(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
,
\gamma_{2,3}\in\Pi(\eta_{2},\eta_{3})
,
R_{1,2}\in{\mathcal{C}}(H_{1},H_{2})
, and
R_{2,3}\in{\mathcal{C}}(H_{2},H_{3})
be arbitrary.
We apply the the gluing lemma (Lemma
1
) and its counterpart for correspondences (Lemma
2
). Let
\gamma
be a gluing of
\gamma_{1,2}
and
\gamma_{1,3}
, and let
\gamma_{1,3}
be its marginal on the first and third coordinates.
Similarly, let
R
be a gluing of
R_{1,2}
and
R_{2,3}
, and let
R_{1,3}
be its image on its first and third components.
We can then write

\displaystyle\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R_{1,3},\gamma_{1,3})=\sup_{(h_{1},h_{3})\in R_{1,3}}

\displaystyle\int\big|\ell_{1}(h_{1}(x_{1}),y_{1})-\ell_{3}(h_{3}(x_{3}),y_{3})\big|\,\gamma_{1,3}(dx_{1}{\times}dy_{1}{\times}dx_{3}{\times}dy_{3})

\displaystyle=\sup_{(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})\in R}

\displaystyle\int\big|\ell_{1}(h_{1}(x_{1}),y_{1})-\ell_{3}(h_{3}(x_{3}),y_{3})\big|\,\gamma(dx_{1}{\times}dy_{1}{\times}dx_{2}{\times}dy_{2}{\times}dx_{3}{\times}dy_{3})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})\in R}

\displaystyle\int\big|\ell_{1}(h_{1}(x_{1}),y_{1})-\ell_{2}(h_{2}(x_{2}),y_{2})\big|\,\gamma(dx_{1}{\times}dy_{1}{\times}dx_{2}{\times}dy_{2}{\times}dx_{3}{\times}dy_{3})

\displaystyle+\sup_{(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})\in R}

\displaystyle\int\big|\ell_{2}(h_{2}(x_{2}),y_{2})-\ell_{3}(h_{3}(x_{3}),y_{3})\big|\,\gamma(dx_{1}{\times}dy_{1}{\times}dx_{2}{\times}dy_{2}{\times}dx_{3}{\times}dy_{3})

\displaystyle=\sup_{(h_{1},h_{2})\in R_{1,2}}

\displaystyle\int\big|\ell_{1}(h_{1}(x_{1}),y_{1})-\ell_{2}(h_{2}(x_{2}),y_{2})\big|\,\gamma_{1,2}(dx_{1}{\times}dy_{1}{\times}dx_{2}{\times}dy_{2})

\displaystyle+\sup_{(h_{2},h_{3})\in R_{2,3}}

\displaystyle\int\big|\ell_{2}(h_{2}(x_{2}),y_{2})-\ell_{3}(h_{3}(x_{3}),y_{3})\big|\,\gamma_{2,3}(dx_{2}{\times}dy_{2}{\times}dx_{3}{\times}dy_{3})

\displaystyle\mkern-100.0mu=\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R_{1,2},\gamma_{1,2})+\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R_{2,3},\gamma_{2,3}).

Since we can construct such a
\gamma_{1,3}
and
R_{1,3}
from any given
\gamma_{1,2},\gamma_{2,3},R_{1,2},R_{2,3}
, we can take the infimum of each side to get the desired inequality.
∎

Theorem
28
.

First we demonstrate that the Risk distance does indeed satisfy Conditions 1 and 2. Let
P^{\prime}
be a simulation of
P
via the maps
f_{1}:X^{\prime}\to X
and
f_{2}:Y^{\prime}\to Y
. Define
f:=f_{1}\times f_{2}
. Since
f_{\sharp}\eta^{\prime}=\eta
,
f
induces a coupling
\gamma_{f}\in\Pi(\eta^{\prime},\eta)
. Define
R\subseteq H^{\prime}\times H
to be all the pairs
(h^{\prime},h)
such that
h\circ f_{1}=f_{2}\circ h^{\prime}
. Since
f
induces a simulation,
R
is a correspondence. By definition of the Risk distance, we can now write

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(h^{\prime},h)\in R}\int\big|\ell^{\prime}(h^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})-\ell(h(x),y)\big|\,\gamma_{f}(dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime}{\times}dx{\times}dy)

\displaystyle=\sup_{(h^{\prime},h)\in R}\int\big|\ell^{\prime}(h^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})-\ell(h\circ f_{1}(x^{\prime}),f_{2}(y^{\prime}))\big|\,\eta^{\prime}(dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime}).

We claim the integrand is identically zero. Indeed,

\displaystyle\ell(h\circ f_{1}(x^{\prime}),f_{2}(y^{\prime}))=\ell(f_{2}\circ h^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),f_{2}(y^{\prime}))=\ell^{\prime}(h^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime}).

Hence
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})=0
.

Let
P=(X,Y,\eta,\ell,H)
and
P^{\prime}=(X,Y,\eta,\ell^{\prime},H)
be any pair of problems that differ only in their loss functions.
We can bound
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})
by selecting the diagonal coupling between
\eta
and itself, and the diagonal correspondence between
H
and itself. This directly yields Condition 2.

Now suppose
d
is a pseudometric satisfying Conditions 1 and 2, and let
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
be problems. Let
\gamma\in\Pi(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
and
R\in{\mathcal{C}}(H_{1},H_{2})
be arbitrary. Define two new problems

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle:=(X_{1}\times X_{2},Y_{1}\times Y_{2},\gamma,\ell^{\prime}_{1},R_{\times})

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle:=(X_{1}\times X_{2},Y_{1}\times Y_{2},\gamma,\ell^{\prime}_{2},R_{\times}),

where

•

\ell^{\prime}_{1}((y_{1},y_{2}),(y^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime}_{2})):=\ell_{1}(y_{1},y^{\prime}_{1})
,

•

\ell^{\prime}_{2}((y_{1},y_{2}),(y^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime}_{2})):=\ell_{2}(y_{2},y^{\prime}_{2})
,

•

R_{\times}:=\left\{h_{1}\times h_{2}\middle|(h_{1},h_{2})\in R\right\}
.

Then
P^{\prime}_{1}
is a simulation of
P_{1}
via the projection maps
X_{1}\times X_{2}\to X_{1}
and
Y_{1}\times Y_{2}\to Y_{1}
, so by Condition 1,
d(P_{1},P^{\prime}_{1})=0
. Similarly,
d(P_{2},P^{\prime}_{2})=0
. Combining this with Condition 2, we get

\displaystyle d(P_{1},P_{2})=d(P^{\prime}_{1},P^{\prime}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\max_{h_{1}\times h_{2}\in R_{\times}}\int\big|\ell^{\prime}_{1}((h_{1}\times h_{2})(x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2}))-\ell^{\prime}_{2}((h_{1}\times h_{2})(x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2}))\big|\,\gamma(dx_{1}{\times}dx_{2}{\times}dy_{1}{\times}dy_{2})

\displaystyle=\max_{(h_{1},h_{2})\in R}\int\big|\ell_{1}(h_{1}(x_{1}),y_{1})-\ell_{2}(h_{2}(x_{2}),y_{2})\big|\,\gamma(dx_{1}{\times}dx_{2}{\times}dy_{1}{\times}dy_{2})

\displaystyle=\dis_{P_{1},P_{2}}(R,\gamma).

Taking an infimum over all choices of
\gamma
and
R
finishes the proof.
∎

29
.

Let
P
and
P^{\prime}
be weakly isomorphic, and let

be a common simulation of both

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{29.6142pt}{-22.20197pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 40.98 -30.72)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Define
\gamma:=m_{\sharp}\eta^{\prime\prime}.
Note that, since the above diagram commutes,
\gamma
is a coupling between
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
. Also, let
\phi:H^{\prime\prime}\to H
be such that
\ell^{\prime\prime}_{h^{\prime\prime}}=\ell_{\phi(h^{\prime\prime})}\circ(f_{1}\times f_{2})
holds
\eta^{\prime\prime}
-almost everywhere. Define
\psi:H^{\prime\prime}\to H^{\prime}
similarly. Then

R:=\left\{(\phi(h^{\prime\prime}),\psi(h^{\prime\prime}))\middle|h^{\prime\prime}\in H^{\prime\prime}\right\}

is a correspondence between
H
and
H^{\prime}
. Finally, compute

\displaystyle\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R,\gamma)

\displaystyle=\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\int\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big|\,\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\int\big|\ell_{h}\circ\pi_{12}(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}\circ\pi_{34}(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big|\,\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\int\big|\ell_{h}\circ\pi_{12}\circ m(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime})-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}\circ\pi_{34}\circ m(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\big|\,\eta^{\prime\prime}(dx^{\prime\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\int\big|\ell_{h}(f_{1}(x^{\prime\prime}),f_{2}(y^{\prime\prime}))-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(g_{1}(x^{\prime\prime}),g_{2}(y^{\prime\prime}))\big|\,\eta^{\prime\prime}(dx^{\prime\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sup_{h^{\prime\prime}\in H^{\prime\prime}}\int\big|\ell_{\phi(h^{\prime\prime})}(f_{1}(x^{\prime\prime}),f_{2}(y^{\prime\prime}))-\ell^{\prime}_{\psi(h^{\prime\prime})}(g_{1}(x^{\prime\prime}),g_{2}(y^{\prime\prime}))\big|\,\eta^{\prime\prime}(dx^{\prime\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sup_{h^{\prime\prime}\in H^{\prime\prime}}\int\big|\ell^{\prime\prime}_{h^{\prime\prime}}(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime})-\ell^{\prime\prime}_{h^{\prime\prime}}(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\big|\,\eta^{\prime\prime}(dx^{\prime\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime\prime})=0.

Now suppose that there exist
R\in{\mathcal{C}}(H,H^{\prime})
and
\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
such that
\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R,\gamma)=0
. Construct a new problem

P^{\prime\prime}=(X\times X^{\prime},Y\times Y^{\prime},\tau_{\sharp}\gamma,\ell^{\prime\prime},R_{\times})

where

•

\tau:X\times Y\times X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}\to X\times X^{\prime}\times Y\times Y^{\prime}
swaps the second and third components,

•

\ell^{\prime\prime}((y_{1},y^{\prime}_{1}),(y_{2},y^{\prime}_{2})):=\ell(y_{1},y_{2})

•

R_{\times}:=\left\{h\times h^{\prime}\middle|(h,h^{\prime})\in R\right\}
.

Then the projection maps
f_{1}:X\times X^{\prime}\to X
and
f_{2}:Y\times Y^{\prime}\to Y
show that
P^{\prime\prime}
is a simulation of
P
. Indeed, since
\gamma
is a coupling of
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle(f_{1}\times f_{2})_{\sharp}\tau_{\sharp}\gamma=((f_{1}\times f_{2})\circ\tau)_{\sharp}\gamma=(\pi_{12})\sharp\gamma=\eta.

Furthermore, for any
h\in H
, we can consider some function
(h\times h^{\prime})\in R_{\times}
and write

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime\prime}_{(h\times h^{\prime})}((x,x^{\prime}),(y,y^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\ell^{\prime\prime}((h(x),h^{\prime}(x^{\prime})),(y,y^{\prime}))=\ell(h(x),y)=\ell_{h}(f_{1}(x,x^{\prime}),f_{2}(y,y^{\prime})).

Similarly,
P^{\prime\prime}
is a simulation of
P^{\prime}
via the projection maps
g_{1}:X\times X^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
and
g_{2}:Y\times Y^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
. Indeed, an almost identical calculation to the one above shows that
(g_{1}\times g_{2})_{\sharp}\tau_{\sharp}\gamma=\eta^{\prime}
. Furthermore, if
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
and
(h\times h^{\prime})\in R_{\times}
, we have already calculated that
\ell^{\prime\prime}_{(h\times h^{\prime})}((x,x^{\prime}),(y,y^{\prime}))=\ell(h(x),y)
.
The fact that
R
and
\gamma
have Risk distortion zero impliex that the latter is equal
\tau_{\sharp}\gamma
-almost everywhere to
\ell^{\prime}(h^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})=\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(g_{1}(x,x^{\prime}),g_{2}(y,y^{\prime}))
, completing the proof.
∎

A.1.1
Connecting the Risk Distance with the Gromov-Wasserstein Distance

Here we discuss a connection with a variant of the Gromov-Wasserstein distance considered by
Hang et al. (2019)
. Given two metric measure spaces
X,X^{\prime}
, a correspondence
R\in{\mathcal{C}}(X,X^{\prime})
and a coupling
\mu\in\Pi(\mu_{X},\mu_{X^{\prime}})
, one considers the following notion of distortion:

\overline{\dis}(R,\mu):=\sup_{(x_{2},x_{2}^{\prime})\in R}\int_{X\times X^{\prime}}\big|d_{X}(x_{1},x_{2})-d_{X^{\prime}}(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})\big|\mu(dx_{1}\times dx_{1}^{\prime}),

which leads to the definition

\overline{d_{\mathrm{GW}}}(X,X^{\prime}):=\inf_{R,\mu}\overline{\dis}(X,X^{\prime}).

This definition, which blends an
L^{\infty}
matching (expressed through the correspondence
R
) with an
L^{1}
one (expressed via the coupling
\mu
) was considered by
Hang et al. (2019)
due its beneficial interaction with stability questions surrounding Fréchet functions in the persistent homology setting.
9
9

9

The precise variant considered in
(
Hang et al., 2019
)
uses “continuous” correspondences. The version we consider here is structurally identical but slightly softer due to our use of arbitrary correspondences.

With the notation and definitions from Section
3.5
, we have the following statement.

Proposition 102
.

For all compact metric measure spaces
X
and
X^{\prime}
:

d_{\mathrm{R}}(i(X),i(X^{\prime}))=\overline{d_{\mathrm{GW}}}(X,X^{\prime}).

Proof.

First note that any correspondence
R
between
H_{X}
and
H_{X^{\prime}}
can be regarded as a correspondence between
X
and
X^{\prime}
. This is so because all functions in
H_{X}
(resp. in
H_{X^{\prime}}
) are constant.

We then have the following calculation:

\displaystyle\dis_{i(X),i(X^{\prime})}(R,\gamma)

\displaystyle=\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\int\big|d_{X}(h(x_{0}),x_{1})-d_{X^{\prime}}(h^{\prime}(x^{\prime}_{0}),x^{\prime}_{1})\big|\,\gamma(dx_{0}{\times}dx_{1}{\times}dx^{\prime}_{0}{\times}dx^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle=\sup_{(x_{2},x^{\prime}_{2})\in R}\int\big|d_{X}(x_{2},x_{1})-d_{X^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}_{2},x^{\prime}_{1})\big|\,\gamma(dx_{0}{\times}dx_{1}{\times}dx^{\prime}_{0}{\times}dx^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle=\sup_{(x_{2},x^{\prime}_{2})\in R}\int\big|d_{X}(x_{2},x_{1})-d_{X^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}_{2},x^{\prime}_{1})\big|\,(\pi_{2,4})_{\sharp}\gamma(dx_{0}{\times}dx_{1}{\times}dx^{\prime}_{0}{\times}dx^{\prime}_{1})

\displaystyle=\overline{\dis}(R,(\pi_{2,4})_{\sharp}\gamma).

The above equality immediately implies that
d_{\mathrm{R}}(i(X),i(X^{\prime}))\geq\overline{d_{\mathrm{GW}}}(X,X^{\prime}).
The reverse inequality can be obtained from the following fact. Let
\tau:X\times X^{\prime}\to X\times X\times X^{\prime}\times X^{\prime}
be the map
(x,x^{\prime})\mapsto(x,x,x^{\prime},x^{\prime})
. Then, if
\mu\in\Pi(\mu_{X},\mu_{X^{\prime}})
,
\gamma_{\mu}:=\tau_{\sharp}\mu
is an element of
\Pi((\Delta_{X})_{\sharp}\mu_{X},(\Delta_{X^{\prime}})_{\sharp}\mu_{X^{\prime}})
. Since
\pi_{2,4}\circ\tau=\mathrm{id}:X\times X^{\prime}\to X\times X^{\prime}
, the identity map,
(\pi_{2,4})_{\sharp}\gamma_{\mu}=\mu
and the calculation above gives
\dis_{i(X),i(X^{\prime})}(R,\gamma_{\mu})=\overline{\dis}(\mu)
. From this we obtain that
d_{\mathrm{R}}(i(X),i(X^{\prime}))\leq\overline{d_{\mathrm{GW}}}(X,X^{\prime}).

∎

Proposition
33
.

If
d_{\mathrm{GW},p}(X,X^{\prime})=0
, then
X
and
X^{\prime}
are isomorphic as metric measure spaces. Using the isomorphism
X\to X^{\prime}
, it is not hard to construct a coupling and correspondence between
i(X)
and
i(X^{\prime})
of risk distortion zero. Conversely, let
d_{\mathrm{R}}(i(X),i(X^{\prime}))=0
. It is not a priori obvious that there exists a correspondence
R\in{\mathcal{C}}(H_{X},H_{X^{\prime}})
and a coupling
\gamma\in\Pi((\Delta_{X})_{\sharp}\mu_{X},(\Delta_{X^{\prime}})_{\sharp}\mu_{X^{\prime}})
that together achieve the infimal risk distortion of zero. In Section
5.2
, we will explore the existence of such couplings and correspondences. In this case, since
d_{X}
and
d_{X^{\prime}}
are continuous functions and
X
and
X^{\prime}
are assumed to be compact, Theorem
60
does guarantee that there exists an
R
and a
\gamma
with
\dis_{i(X),i(X^{\prime})}(R,\gamma)=0
. Furthermore, recall that a correspondence
R
between
H_{X}
and
H_{X^{\prime}}
is equivalent to a correspondence between
X
and
X^{\prime}
. Hence, as in the proof of Proposition
102
, we have
0=\dis_{i(X),i(X^{\prime})}(R,\gamma)=\overline{\dis}(R,(\pi_{2,4})_{\sharp}\gamma)
. Set
R^{\prime}:=\operatorname{supp}((\pi_{2,4})_{\sharp}\gamma)
. Since the integrand defining
\overline{\dis}(R,(\pi_{2,4})_{\sharp}\gamma)
is continuous and non-negative, we can conclude that

d_{X}(x_{2},x_{1})=d_{X^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}_{2},x^{\prime}_{1})

(3)

for all
(x_{2},x^{\prime}_{2})\in R
and
(x_{1},x^{\prime}_{1})\in R^{\prime}
. Furthermore, since
(\pi_{2,4})_{\sharp}\gamma
is a coupling between the fully supported measures
\mu_{X}
and
\mu_{X^{\prime}}
, its support
R^{\prime}
is a correspondence between
X
and
X^{\prime}

(see
Mémoli, 2011
, Lemma 2.2)
.
We claim that
R=R^{\prime}
. Indeed, if
(x_{2},x^{\prime}_{2})\in R
, selecting any pair
(x_{2},x^{\prime}_{1})\in R^{\prime}
yields
d_{X}(x_{2},x_{2})=d_{X^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}_{2},x^{\prime}_{1})
and hence
x^{\prime}_{2}=x^{\prime}_{1}
. This shows
R\subseteq R^{\prime}
, and a similar argument shows the opposite inclusion. We furthermore claim that
R
is the graph of a bijection. Let
(x,x^{\prime}),(x,x^{\prime\prime})\in R
. Then
d_{X}(x,x)=d_{X^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})
, showing
x^{\prime}=x^{\prime\prime}
. Again, a similar argument holds when reversing the roles of
X
and
X^{\prime}
.
Let
f:X\to X^{\prime}
be the bijection whose graph is
R
. Then equation (
3
) becomes
d_{X}(x_{2},x_{1})=d_{X^{\prime}}(f(x_{2}),f(x_{1}))
, showing that
f
is an isometry. Additionally, since
(\pi_{2,4})_{\sharp}\gamma
is a coupling of
\mu_{X}
and
\mu_{X^{\prime}}
which is supported on
R
, the projections
R\to X
and
R\to X^{\prime}
are measure-preserving bijections. It follows that the composition of the natural maps
X\to R\to X^{\prime}
, which agrees with
f
, is a measure-preserving map. Hence
f
is an isomorphism of metric measure spaces, and
d_{\mathrm{GW},1}(X,X^{\prime})=0
.
∎

A.2
Proofs from Section
4

Theorem
37
.

Let
\prob_{1}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})\subseteq\prob({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})
be the subset of probability measures with finite mean. The
\mathrm{mean}
function

\displaystyle\mathrm{mean}:\prob_{1}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})

\displaystyle\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}

is 1-Lipschitz when the domain is endowed with the 1-Wasserstein distance
d_{\mathrm{W},1}
and the codomain is given the Euclidean distance. We will now “lift” the
\mathrm{mean}
function to a function on power sets by applying it elementwise. That is, we define

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pow}(\mathrm{mean}):\mathrm{Pow}(\prob_{1}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}))

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Pow}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})

\displaystyle\left\{\alpha_{i}\middle|i\in I\right\}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{\mathrm{mean}(\alpha_{i})\middle|i\in I\right\}.

We also endow the domain and codomain with the Hausdorff distance, where the underlying metrics are
d_{\mathrm{W},1}
and the Euclidean distance respectively. Since the
\mathrm{mean}
function is 1-Lipschitz, so is
\mathrm{Pow}(\mathrm{mean})
. The infimum function
\inf:\mathrm{Pow}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
1-Lipschitz as well, so the composition
\inf\circ\mathrm{Pow}(\mathrm{mean})
is 1-Lipschitz. In other words, for any
M,N\subseteq\prob_{1}({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})
, we have

\big|\inf\left\{\mathrm{mean}(\mu)\middle|\mu\in M\right\}-\inf\left\{\mathrm{mean}(\nu)\middle|\nu\in N\right\}\big|\leq d_{\mathrm{H}}^{d_{\mathrm{W},1}}(M,N).

By Proposition
36
, applying this inequality when
M
and
N
are loss profile sets gives the first desired inequality.

To prove the second inequality, observe that
d(P,P^{\prime}):=d_{\mathrm{H}}^{d_{\mathrm{W},1}}(L(P),L(P^{\prime}))
is a pseudometric on the collection of all problems. By Theorem
28
, we need only show that
d
satisfies Conditions 1 and 2. Beginning with Condition 1, let
P^{\prime}
be a simulation of
P
via the maps
f_{1}:X^{\prime}\to X
and
f_{2}:Y^{\prime}\to Y
. Let
f:=f_{1}\times f_{2}
. If
h\in H
and
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
are such that
f_{2}\circ h=h^{\prime}\circ f_{2}
, then

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}\circ f(x,y)

\displaystyle=\ell^{\prime}(h^{\prime}\circ f_{1}(x),f_{2}(y))=\ell^{\prime}(f_{2}\circ h(x),f_{2}(y))=\ell(h(x),y)=\ell_{h}(x,y).

Hence the loss profiles of
h
and
h^{\prime}
are equal:

\displaystyle(\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}})_{\sharp}\eta^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}\circ f)_{\sharp}\eta=(\ell_{h})_{\sharp}\eta.

Such an
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
exists for every
h\in H
and vice versa by definition of a simulation, so we conclude that
L(P)=L(P^{\prime})
and
d(P,P^{\prime})=0
.

For Condition 2, let
h\in H
. We can bound the Wasserstein distance between the loss profiles
(\ell_{h})_{\sharp}\eta
and
(\ell^{\prime}_{h})_{\sharp}\eta
by selecting the coupling
(\ell_{h},\ell^{\prime}_{h})_{\sharp}\eta
, which yields

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{W},1}((\ell_{h})_{\sharp}\eta,(\ell^{\prime}_{h})_{\sharp}\eta)

\displaystyle\leq\int\big|r-s\big|(\ell_{h},\ell^{\prime}_{h})_{\sharp}\eta(dr{\times}ds)

\displaystyle=\int\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell^{\prime}_{h}(x,y)\big|\,\eta(dx\times dy).

Since this holds for all
h
, Condition 2 is satisfied as well.
∎

Theorem
41
.

Let
R\in{\mathcal{C}}(H,H^{\prime})
and
\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
be arbitrary. Then

\displaystyle\big|R_{m}(P)-R_{m}(P^{\prime})\big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg|\int\int\sup_{h\in H}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}\ell_{h}(x_{i},y_{i})\,\mathrm{Rad}^{\otimes m}(d\sigma)\,\eta^{\otimes m}(d\overline{x}\times d\overline{y})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int\int\sup_{h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})\,\mathrm{Rad}^{\otimes m}(d\sigma)\,\eta^{\otimes m}(d\overline{x}^{\prime}\times d\overline{y}^{\prime})\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int\int\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\left|\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}\ell_{h}(x_{i},y_{i})-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})\right|\,\mathrm{Rad}^{\otimes m}(d\sigma)\,\gamma^{\otimes m}(d\overline{x}{\times}d\overline{y}{\times}d\overline{x}^{\prime}{\times}d\overline{y}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\big|\ell_{h}(x_{i},y_{i})-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})\big|\,\gamma^{\otimes m}(d\overline{x}{\times}d\overline{y}{\times}d\overline{x}^{\prime}{\times}d\overline{y}^{\prime}).

Let
\widehat{\gamma}
be the unique probability measure on the countable product
(X\times Y)^{\mathbb{N}}
whose marginal on the first
m
components is
\gamma^{\otimes m}
. Then the above integral can be written

\int\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\big|\ell_{h}(x_{i},y_{i})-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})\big|\,\widehat{\gamma}(d\overline{x}{\times}d\overline{y}{\times}d\overline{x}^{\prime}{\times}d\overline{y}^{\prime}).

(4)

We now aim to establish the bound

\displaystyle\int\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\big|\ell_{h}(x_{i},y_{i})-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})\big|\,\widehat{\gamma}(d\overline{x}{\times}d\overline{y}{\times}d\overline{x}^{\prime}{\times}d\overline{y}^{\prime})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\int\big|\ell_{h}(x_{i},y_{i})-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})\big|\gamma(d\overline{x}{\times}d\overline{y}{\times}d\overline{x}^{\prime}{\times}d\overline{y}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\mathrm{Rad}_{m}(\mathcal{F})

since infimizing this inequality over
\gamma
and
R
will give us the result.
It will suffice to establish

\displaystyle\int\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\left[\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\big|\ell_{h}(x_{i},y_{i})-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})\big|-\int\big|\ell_{h}(x_{i},y_{i})-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})\big|\right]\,\widehat{\gamma}(d\overline{x}{\times}d\overline{y}{\times}d\overline{x}^{\prime}{\times}d\overline{y}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathrm{Rad}_{m}(\mathcal{F}),

which, in probabilistic notation, is written

{\mathbb{E}}\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\left[\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\big|\ell_{h}(X_{i},Y_{i})-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}_{i},Y^{\prime}_{i})\big|-{\mathbb{E}}\big|\ell_{h}(X,Y)-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\big|\right]\leq 2\mathrm{Rad}_{m}(\mathcal{F})

(5)

where the first expectation is taken over the tuples
(X_{i},Y_{i},X^{\prime}_{i},Y^{\prime}_{i})
each sampled i.i.d. from
\gamma
, and the second expectation is over
(X,Y,X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
also sampled from
\gamma
independently of the other tuples. The expression on the left hand side of Equation
5
is well-studied in the statistical learning theory literature, and the bound in equation
5
is easily obtained by a symmetrization argument like the one laid out in
(
Liao, 2020
, Theorem 4.1)
.

Now assume
\mathcal{F}
is a universal Glivenko-Cantelli class. Return to the expression (
4
) and let
m\to\infty
.
By the Glivenko-Cantelli assumption, the integrand of (
4
) converges almost everywhere to

\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\int\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big|\,\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})=\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R,\gamma).

Furthermore, since
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
are bounded, by the dominated convergence theorem, the limit of (
4
) itself is
\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R,\gamma)
as well. Infimizing over the choices of
R
and
\gamma
completes the proof.
∎

Lemma
48
.

We aim to bound
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})
. To this end, define

\displaystyle\Delta:X\times Y\times Y

\displaystyle\to X\times Y\times X\times Y

\displaystyle(x,y,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mapsto(x,y,x,y^{\prime}).

Let
\Pi_{g}
be the collection of all gluings of
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
along
X
. That is,

\displaystyle\Pi_{g}:=\left\{\lambda\in\prob(X\times Y\times Y)\middle|(\pi_{1,2})_{\sharp}\lambda=\eta,(\pi_{1,3})_{\sharp}\lambda=\eta^{\prime}\right\}.

By the gluing lemma (Lemma
1
),
\Pi_{g}
is nonempty.
Note that if
\lambda\in\Pi_{g}
, then
\Delta_{\sharp}\lambda\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
.

We can then write

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell(h(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\big|\,\gamma(dx\times dy\times dx^{\prime}\times dy^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\lambda\in\Pi_{g}}\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell(h(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\big|\,\Delta_{\sharp}\lambda(dx\times dy\times dx^{\prime}\times dy^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\lambda\in\Pi_{g}}\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell(h(x),y^{\prime})\big|\,\lambda(dx\times dy\times dy^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq C\inf_{\lambda\in\Pi_{g}}\int d_{Y}(y,y^{\prime})\,\lambda(dx\times dy\times dy^{\prime}).

Now suppose
\tau\in\Pi(\beta,\beta^{\prime})
. Then one can show that the induced coupling
\lambda\in\prob(X\times Y\times Y)
, which has
X
-marginal
\alpha
and
X
-disintegration
\tau:X\to\prob(Y\times Y^{\prime})
, lies in
\Pi_{g}
. Hence we can continue our string of inequalities with

\displaystyle\leq C\inf_{\tau\in\Pi(\beta,\beta^{\prime})}\int\int d_{Y}(y,y^{\prime})\,\tau(x)(dy\times dy^{\prime})\alpha(dx)

\displaystyle=Cd_{\mathrm{W},1}^{d_{Y}}(\beta,\beta^{\prime}),

the desired bound.
∎

Theorem
49
.

By Lemma
48
, we obtain

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})\leq Cd_{\mathrm{W},1}^{d_{Y}}(\beta,\beta^{\prime}).

We can write
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
as

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=\Delta_{X}\cdot(\delta_{\id_{X}}\otimes\beta)\cdot\delta_{\pi_{2}}

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{X}\cdot(\delta_{\id_{X}}\otimes\beta)\cdot N.

Here
\Delta_{X}
is the diagonal kernel
\Delta_{X}:X\to X\times X
given by
\Delta_{X}(x)=\delta_{(x,x)}
, and
\otimes
represents the product of Markov kernels.
Note that

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot\Delta_{X}\cdot(\delta_{\id_{X}}\otimes\beta)=\eta.

Hence
\Delta_{X}\cdot(\delta_{\id_{X}}\otimes\beta)
is an example of a measure non-increasing Markov kernel
(
Patterson, 2020
, Proposition 6.1)
, so we can write

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{d_{Y}}(\Delta_{X}\cdot(\delta_{\id_{X}}\otimes\beta)\cdot\delta_{\pi_{2}},\,\Delta_{X}\cdot(\delta_{\id_{X}}\otimes\beta)\cdot N)\leq d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{d_{Y}}(\delta_{\pi_{2}},N),

completing the proof.
∎

A.3
Proofs from Section
5

Lemma
58
.

The following proof uses ideas similar to those in the proof of Theorem 4.1 of
Villani (2008)
, with the additional wrinkle of an integrand that depends on
n
and an additional trick of “hot-swapping” an integrand with a lower semi-continuous version.

Since the topology on the domain is induced by a pseudometric, it is enough to consider an arbitrary convergent sequence
(h_{n},h^{\prime}_{n},\gamma_{n})\to(h,h^{\prime},\gamma)
in
H\times H^{\prime}\times\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
and check that,

\widetilde{\dis}_{P,P^{\prime}}(h,h^{\prime},\gamma)\leq\liminf_{n}\widetilde{\dis}_{P,P^{\prime}}(h_{n},h^{\prime}_{n},\gamma_{n}).

To that end, let
\varepsilon>0
be arbitrary. We define a function

\displaystyle f:X\times Y\times X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}

\displaystyle\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}

\displaystyle(x_{0},y_{0},x^{\prime}_{0},y^{\prime}_{0})

\displaystyle\mapsto\liminf_{(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\to(x_{0},y_{0},x^{\prime}_{0},y^{\prime}_{0})}|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})|.

That is,
f
is a lower semi-continuous version of the function
|\ell_{h}-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}|
, constructed by modifying the latter on its points of discontinuity. Indeed,
f
and
|\ell_{h}-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}|
agree wherever the latter is continuous, which is
\gamma
-almost everywhere on
X\times Y\times X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}
. To wit, if
A\subseteq X\times Y
and
A^{\prime}\subseteq X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}
are the points at which
\ell_{h}
and
\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}
are discontinuous respectively, then the points of discontinuity for
|\ell_{h}-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}|
lie within
A\times X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}\cup X\times Y\times A^{\prime}
, and one can check

\displaystyle\gamma(A\times X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}\cup X\times Y\times A^{\prime})\leq\gamma(A\times X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime})+\gamma(X\times Y\times A^{\prime})=\eta(A)+\eta^{\prime}(A^{\prime})=0.

Hence
f
is a lower semi-continuous function which agrees
\gamma
-almost everywhere with
|\ell_{h}-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}|
.

We can now proceed with our convergence argument; let
\varepsilon>0
and pick
N
large enough that, for all
n\geq N
,

•

d_{\ell,\eta}(h_{n},h)<\varepsilon/3
,

•

d_{\ell^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(h^{\prime}_{n},h^{\prime})<\varepsilon/3
,

•

and
10
10

10

In this proof, for conciseness, we will write
\int f(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})d\gamma
instead of
\int f(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\,\gamma(dx\times dy\times dx^{\prime}\times dy^{\prime})
, etc.

\int f(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\,d\gamma<\int f(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\,d\gamma_{n}+\varepsilon/3.

The latter is possible since
f
is a lower semi-continuous, non-negative function
(
Villani, 2008
, Lemma 4.3)
.
Then for large
n
we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\dis}_{P,P^{\prime}}(h,h^{\prime},\gamma)

\displaystyle=\int\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big|\,d\gamma

\displaystyle=\int f(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\,d\gamma

\displaystyle\leq\int f(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\,d\gamma_{n}+\varepsilon/3

\displaystyle=\int\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big|\,d\gamma_{n}+\varepsilon/3

\displaystyle\leq\int\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell_{h_{n}}(x,y)\big|\,d\gamma_{n}

\displaystyle+\int\big|\ell_{h_{n}}(x,y)-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}_{n}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big|\,d\gamma_{n}

\displaystyle+\int\big|\ell_{h^{\prime}_{n}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big|\,d\gamma_{n}+\varepsilon/3

\displaystyle=d_{\ell,\eta}(h,h_{n})+\widetilde{\dis}_{P,P^{\prime}}(h_{n},h^{\prime}_{n},\gamma_{n})+d_{\ell^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(h^{\prime},h^{\prime}_{n})+\varepsilon/3<\widetilde{\dis}_{P,P^{\prime}}(h_{n},h^{\prime}_{n},\gamma_{n})+\varepsilon

as desired.
In the last equality, we replace
\gamma_{n}
with
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
in the first and last integrals respectively since
\gamma_{n}
is a coupling between
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
.
∎

In order to prove Lemma
59
we will require the following technical lemma.

Lemma 103
.

Let
X
be a pseudometric space and
f:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
a lower semi-continuous function. Then the map

\displaystyle F:\mathrm{Pow}(X)

\displaystyle\to{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto\sup_{a\in A}f(a)

is lower semi-continuous as well when the power set
\mathrm{Pow}(X)
is endowed with the Hausdorff metric.

Proof.

Let the sequence of subsets
A_{n}\subseteq X
converge to
A\subseteq X
in the Hausdorff distance. Select
a^{\prime}\in A
such that

\sup_{a\in A}f(a)<f(a^{\prime})+\varepsilon/2.

Select also a sequence
a^{\prime}_{n}\in X
such that
a^{\prime}_{n}\in A_{n}
for all
n
, and
a^{\prime}_{n}
converges to
a^{\prime}
. Then for sufficiently large
n
,

f(a^{\prime})<f(a^{\prime}_{n})+\varepsilon/2

by lower semi-continuity of
f
. Hence for large
n
,

\displaystyle F(A)=\sup_{a\in A}f(a)<f(a^{\prime})+\varepsilon/2<f(a^{\prime}_{n})+\varepsilon\leq\sup_{a\in A_{n}}f(a)+\varepsilon=F(A_{n})+\varepsilon.

Hence
F
is lower semi-continuous.
∎

Lemma
59
.

Write

\displaystyle\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R,\gamma)=\sup_{((h,\nu),(h^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}))\in R\times\{\gamma\}}\widetilde{\dis}_{P,P^{\prime}}(h,h^{\prime},\nu).

Using the above equation, we can think of
\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}
as a composition of two maps:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}(H,H^{\prime})\times\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Pow}(H\times H^{\prime}\times\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime}))

\displaystyle(R,\gamma)

\displaystyle\mapsto R\times\{\gamma\},

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pow}(H\times H^{\prime}\times\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\to{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto\sup_{(h,h^{\prime},\nu)\in A}\widetilde{\dis}_{P,P^{\prime}}(h,h^{\prime},\nu).

The second map is lower semi-continuous by Lemma
103
. The first map is an isometric embedding. Indeed, let
d_{w}
be a metric on
\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
that induces the weak topology. The topology on the domain
{\mathcal{C}}(H,H^{\prime})\times\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
is induced by the metric

\displaystyle d_{1}((R_{1},\gamma_{1}),(R_{2},\gamma_{2})):=\max\{d_{\mathrm{H}}(R_{1},R_{2}),d_{w}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\}.

Meanwhile, unwrapping the metric on the codomain
\mathrm{Pow}(H\times H^{\prime}\times\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime}))
gives us

\displaystyle d_{2}(R_{1}\times\{\gamma_{1}\},R_{2}\times\{\gamma_{2}\})

\displaystyle:=\max\Bigg\{

\displaystyle\sup_{(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime},\nu_{1})\in R_{1}\times\{\gamma_{1}\}}\inf_{(h_{2},h_{2}^{\prime},\nu_{2})\in R_{2}\times\{\gamma_{2}\}}\max\{d_{H\times H^{\prime}}((h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime})(h_{2},h_{2}^{\prime})),d_{w}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\},

\displaystyle\sup_{(h_{2},h_{2}^{\prime},\nu_{2})\in R_{2}\times\{\gamma_{2}\}}\inf_{(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime},\nu_{1})\in R_{1}\times\{\gamma_{1}\}}\max\{d_{H\times H^{\prime}}((h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime})(h_{2},h_{2}^{\prime})),d_{w}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\max\Bigg\{

\displaystyle\sup_{(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime})\in R_{1}}\inf_{(h_{2},h_{2}^{\prime})\in R_{2}}d_{H\times H^{\prime}}((h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime})(h_{2},h_{2}^{\prime})),

\displaystyle\sup_{(h_{2},h_{2}^{\prime})\in R_{2}}\inf_{(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime})\in R_{1}}d_{H\times H^{\prime}}((h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime})(h_{2},h_{2}^{\prime})),d_{w}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\max\{

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{H}}(R_{1},R_{2}),d_{w}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\},

which is the definition of
d_{1}((R_{1},\gamma_{1}),(R_{2},\gamma_{2}))
. Hence the composition is lower semi-continuous.
∎

Theorem
60
.

Throughout this proof, let
{\mathcal{C}}_{c}(H,H^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}(H,H^{\prime})
be the collection of topologically closed correspondences between
H
and
H^{\prime}
.

Note that
\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
is compact under the weak topology, since
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
are probability measures on Polish spaces. Compactness of such coupling spaces is proved, for instance, by
Sturm (2023, Lemma 1.2)
,
Chowdhury and Mémoli (2019, Lemma 2.2)
and in a proof by
Villani (2003, Prop 2.1)
. Similarly,
{\mathcal{C}}_{c}(H,H^{\prime})
is compact under the Hausdorff distance when
H
and
H^{\prime}
are compact. (Here we are equipping
H
and
H^{\prime}
with the pseudometrics
d_{\ell,\eta}
and
d_{\ell^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}
respectively, introduced in Section
4.5
.) As noted by
Chowdhury and Mémoli (2018, Section 2)
, compactness of such correspondence spaces follows from a theorem of Blaschke
(
Burago et al., 2001
, Theorem 7.3.8)
. Indeed, Blaschke’s theorem implies that the space of all closed subsets of
H\times H^{\prime}
is compact. Since
{\mathcal{C}}_{c}(H,H^{\prime})
is closed in the set of all closed subsets of
H\times H^{\prime}
, being the intersection of the preimages of
H
and
H^{\prime}
under the projection maps onto the first and second coordinates respectively, we get that
{\mathcal{C}}_{c}(H,H^{\prime})
is compact as well.

The lower semi-continuity of
\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}
(Lemma
59
) in particular implies that for any correspondence
R
and coupling
\gamma
,
\dis(R,\gamma)=\dis(\mathrm{cl}(R),\gamma)
, where
\mathrm{cl}
represents topological closure. Indeed, since
R
and its closure are distance zero apart in the Hausdorff distance, the constant sequence
R
converges to
\mathrm{cl}(R)
and vice versa. Hence, when computing the Risk distance, we can restrict our attention to closed correspondences:

d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in{\mathcal{C}}(H,H^{\prime})\\
\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R,\gamma)=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in{\mathcal{C}}_{c}(H,H^{\prime})\\
\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}(R,\gamma).

Since
\dis_{P,P^{\prime}}
is lower semi-continuous, and the latter infimum is over a pair of compact sets, the infimum must be achieved.
∎

Theorem
65
.

First consider an arbitrary finite partition
Q
of
Y
. Let
\pi:Y\to Q
be the quotient map. Since
(\id_{X}\times\pi)_{\sharp}\eta=\eta_{Q}
, the map
\id_{X}\times\pi
induces a coupling
\gamma_{(\id_{X}\times\pi)}
between
\eta
and
\eta_{Q}
. We also define a correspondence
R_{\pi}
between
H
and
H_{Q}
by pairing each
h\in H
with
\pi\circ h\in H_{Q}
. Selecting this coupling and correspondence gives us a bound on the Risk distance:

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P_{Q})

\displaystyle\leq\dis_{P,P_{Q}}(R_{\pi},\gamma_{\id_{X}\times\pi})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(h,\pi\circ h)\in R_{\pi}}\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell^{\prime}(\pi\circ h(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\big|\,\gamma_{(\id_{X}\times\pi)}(dx\times dy\times dx^{\prime}\times dy^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sup_{h\in H}\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell^{\prime}(\pi\circ h(x),\pi(y))\big|\,\eta(dx\times dy)

\displaystyle=\sup_{h\in H}\int\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}\in\pi\circ h(x)\\
y^{\prime\prime}\in\pi(y)\end{subarray}}\ell(y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})-\ell(h(x),y)\,\eta(dx\times dy)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{h\in H}\int\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}\in\pi\circ h(x)\\
y^{\prime\prime}\in\pi(y)\end{subarray}}\ell(y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})-\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}\in\pi\circ h(x)\\
y^{\prime\prime}\in\pi(y)\end{subarray}}\ell(y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\,\eta(dx\times dy)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{h\in H}\sup_{(x,y)\in X\times Y}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}\in\pi\circ h(x)\\
y^{\prime\prime}\in\pi(y)\end{subarray}}\ell(y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})-\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}\in\pi\circ h(x)\\
y^{\prime\prime}\in\pi(y)\end{subarray}}\ell(y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\max_{q,q^{\prime}\in Q}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}\in q^{\prime}\\
y^{\prime\prime}\in q\end{subarray}}\ell(y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})-\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}\in q^{\prime}\\
y^{\prime\prime}\in q\end{subarray}}\ell(y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\right].

(6)

The bound (
6
) holds for an arbitrary finite partition
Q
of
Y
.

Since
P
is a compact problem, we can pick a metric
d_{Y}
on
Y
with respect to which
Y
is compact. (Recall that we required our response spaces to be Polish, which makes
Y
metrizable.) In particular,
Y\times Y
is a compact metric space under the product metric, given by

d_{Y\times Y}((y_{1},y_{1}^{\prime}),(y_{2},y_{2}^{\prime})):=\max\{d_{Y}(y_{1},y_{2}),d_{Y}(y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime})\}.

By compactness,
\ell
is uniformly continuous with respect to
d_{Y\times Y}
. Hence we can pick
\delta>0
such that, if
y_{1},y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2},y_{2}^{\prime}\in Y
and

d_{Y\times Y}((y_{1},y_{1}^{\prime}),(y_{2},y_{2}^{\prime}))<\delta,

then

\big|\ell(y_{1},y_{1}^{\prime})-\ell(y_{2},y_{2}^{\prime})\big|<\varepsilon.

Again using compactness, we choose a partition
Q
of
Y
such that
\diam_{d_{Y}}(q)<\delta
for all
q\in Q
. Then for any
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
,
y_{1},y_{2}\in q
,
y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime}\in q^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle d_{Y\times Y}((y_{1},y_{1}^{\prime}),(y_{2},y_{2}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\max\{d_{Y}(y_{1},y_{2}),d_{Y}(y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime})\}<\delta.

Hence

\displaystyle\big|\ell(y_{1},y_{1}^{\prime})-\ell(y_{2},y_{2}^{\prime})\big|<\varepsilon.

Taking a supremum over all choices of
q,q^{\prime},y_{1},y_{2},y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime}
, combined with inequality (
6
), gives us the result.
∎

Theorem
69
.

We will proceed by approximating
P
and
P_{n}
by problems with finite input and output spaces. The process of approximation will proceed in steps depicted as follows:

\displaystyle P\xrightarrow{\text{Restrict to finite $H$}}P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\text{Replace $Y$ with partition $Q_{Y}$}}P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}\xrightarrow{\text{Replace $X$ with partition $Q_{X}$}}P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},Q_{X}}

A similar sequence of approximations will be constructed for the empirical problem
P_{n}
to produce
P_{Q_{Y},Q_{X},n}
. We then show that
P_{Q_{Y},Q_{X},n}
converges a.s. to
P_{Q_{Y},Q_{X}}
.

To wit, let
\varepsilon>0
and construct new problems as follows. Since
H
is compact, we can find a finite
\varepsilon
-net
H^{\prime}\subseteq H
. Define

\displaystyle P^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=(X,Y,\eta,\ell,H^{\prime})

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{n}

\displaystyle:=(X,Y,\eta_{n},\ell,H^{\prime}).

By Theorem
65
, we can select a partition
Q_{Y}
of
Y
such that the resulting coarsened problem
P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}
is
\varepsilon
-close to
P^{\prime}
. Furthermore, by Remark
66
, we can choose
Q_{Y}
in a way that does not depend on the joint law
\eta
. We can coarsen
P^{\prime}_{n}
with respect to the same partition
Q_{Y}
, giving us two new problems:

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}

\displaystyle:=(X,Q_{Y},(\id_{X}\times\pi_{Q_{Y}})_{\sharp}\eta,\ell_{Q_{Y}},H^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}})

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},n}

\displaystyle:=(X,Q_{Y},(\id_{X}\times\pi_{Q_{Y}})_{\sharp}\eta_{n},\ell_{Q_{Y}},H^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}).

Recall that
\pi_{Q_{Y}}:Y\to Q_{Y}
is the canonical quotient map,
\ell_{Q_{Y}}(q,q^{\prime}):=\sup_{y\in q,z\in q^{\prime}}\ell(q,q^{\prime})
, and
H^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}:=\{\pi_{Q_{Y}}\circ h|h\in H^{\prime}\}
.

For the third and final round of approximation, notice that

A:=\{h^{-1}(q)|h\in H^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}},q\in Q_{Y}\}

is a finite collection of subsets of
X
. Let
Q_{X}
be the partition of
X
generated by
A
. That is,
Q_{X}
is the partition of
X
induced by the equivalence relation
{\sim}
where
x\sim x^{\prime}
means that
h(x)=h(x^{\prime})
for all
h\in H^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}
. Then define

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},Q_{X}}

\displaystyle:=(Q_{X},Q_{Y},(\pi_{Q_{X}}\times\pi_{Q_{Y}})_{\sharp}\eta,\ell_{Q_{Y}},\widetilde{H^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}})

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},Q_{X},n}

\displaystyle:=(Q_{X},Q_{Y},(\pi_{Q_{X}}\times\pi_{Q_{Y}})_{\sharp}\eta_{n},\ell_{Q_{Y}},\widetilde{H^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}}),

where
\pi_{Q_{X}}:X\to Q_{X}
is the canonical quotient map, and
\widetilde{H^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}}
is the collection of functions sending
\pi_{Q_{X}}(x)\mapsto h(x)
as
h
ranges over
H^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}
.

We apply the triangle inequality to write

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P_{n})\leq

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})

(1)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime},P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}})

(2)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}},P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},Q_{X}})

(3)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},Q_{X}},P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},Q_{X},n})

(4)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},Q_{X},n},P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},n})

(5)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},n},P^{\prime}_{n})

(6)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime}_{n},P_{n}).

(7)

We consider each of these terms individually.

1.

Since
P
and
P^{\prime}
have the same joint law, we can bound
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})
above by selecting the diagonal coupling. Additionally, since
H^{\prime}
is an
\varepsilon
-net for
H
, we can select a correspondence
R\in{\mathcal{C}}(H,H^{\prime})
such that
d_{\ell,\eta}(h,h^{\prime})<\varepsilon
for all
(h,h^{\prime})\in R
. This produces the bound

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell(h^{\prime}(x),y)\big|\,\eta(dx\times dy)=\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}d_{\ell,\eta}(h,h^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon.

2.

We chose
Q_{Y}
so that
P^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}
would be
\varepsilon
-close under
d_{\mathrm{R}}
, so this term is at most
\varepsilon
.

3.

We bound
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}},P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},Q_{X}})
by selecting the coupling induced by
\pi_{Q_{X}}\times\id_{Q_{Y}}:X\times Y\to Q_{X}\times Y
. We also select the correspondence
R
between
H^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}
and
\widetilde{H^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}}
that pairs each
h\in H^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}
with its counterpart
h^{\prime}\in\widetilde{H^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}}
which sends
\pi_{Q_{X}}(x)\mapsto h(x)
. Then we obtain the bound

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}},P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},Q_{X}})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\int\big|\ell(h(x),q)-\ell(h^{\prime}(\pi_{Q_{X}}(x)),q)\big|\,(\pi_{Q_{X}}\times\id_{Q_{Y}})_{\sharp}\eta(dx\times dq)

\displaystyle=\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\int\big|\ell(h(x),q)-\ell(h(x)),q)\big|\,(\pi_{Q_{X}}\times\id_{Q_{Y}})_{\sharp}\eta(dx\times dq)=0.

Alternatively, one could note that
P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y}}
is a simulation of
P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},Q_{X}}
via the maps
\pi_{Q_{X}}
and
\id_{Q_{Y}}
and apply Theorem
28
.

4.

For brevity, define

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle:=(\pi_{Q_{X}}\times\pi_{Q_{Y}})_{\sharp}\eta

\displaystyle\nu_{n}

\displaystyle:=(\pi_{Q_{X}}{\times}\pi_{Q_{Y}})_{\sharp}\eta_{n}

We apply the total variation bound from Proposition
44
to get

d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},Q_{X}},P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},Q_{X},n})\leq\ell_{\mathsf{max}}d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\nu,\nu_{n}).

Note that
\nu_{n}
is the empirical measure for
\nu
, which is a measure supported on a finite set. Hence by the law of large numbers, the total variation distance between
\nu
and
\nu_{n}
converges to 0 a.s. as
n\to\infty
. Hence, with probability 1, for sufficiently large
n
, we have
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},Q_{X}},P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},Q_{X},n})<\varepsilon
.

5.

The same calculation we applied to term (3) shows that
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},Q_{X},n},P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},n})=0
.

6.

Since we chose
Q_{Y}
in a way that did not depend on the joint law
\eta
, and since
P^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}_{n}
only differ in their joint laws, we get
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime}_{n},P^{\prime}_{Q_{Y},n})<\varepsilon
.

7.

Consider the (random) function on
H\times H
given by

d_{\ell,\eta_{n}}(h,h^{\prime}):=\int\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell_{h^{\prime}}(x,y)\big|\,\eta_{n}(dx{\times}dy).

Let
R
be the same as in our calculations for term (1). Then we can write

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime}_{n},P_{n})\leq\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\int\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell_{h^{\prime}}(x,y)\,\big|\,\eta_{n}(dx\times dy)=\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}d_{\ell,\eta_{n}}(h,h^{\prime}).

Furthermore, since we assumed the integrands
|\ell_{h}-\ell_{h^{\prime}}|
are a Glivenko-Cantelli class with respect to
\eta
, we have that for sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}d_{\ell,\eta_{n}}(h,h^{\prime})-\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}d_{\ell,\eta_{n}}(h,h^{\prime})-d_{\ell,\eta}(h,h^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\big|d_{\ell,\eta_{n}}(h,h^{\prime})-d_{\ell,\eta}(h,h^{\prime})\big|<\varepsilon.

Here the Glivenko-Cantelli assumption is used in the last inequality. Hence

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P^{\prime}_{n},P_{n})\leq\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}d_{\ell,\eta_{n}}(h,h^{\prime})<2\varepsilon.

Combining our bounds for all seven terms gives us that, with probability 1, for sufficiently large
n
we have

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P_{n})<\varepsilon+\varepsilon+0+\varepsilon+0+\varepsilon+2\varepsilon=6\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon
was arbitrary, we conclude that
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P_{n})\to 0
with probability 1.
∎

A.4
Proofs from Section
