Proposition
79
.

Let
(X,d_{X},\mu_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y},\mu_{Y})
be two metric measure spaces. The map

\displaystyle\Pi((\Delta_{X})_{\sharp}\mu_{X},(\Delta_{Y})_{\sharp}\mu_{Y})

\displaystyle\to\Pi(\mu_{X},\mu_{Y})

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle\mapsto(\pi_{2,4})_{\sharp}\gamma

is a bijection. Here,
\pi_{2,4}:X\times X\times Y\times Y\to X\times Y
is the map
(x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime})\mapsto(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
. Using this fact, we can write

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R},1}(P_{X},P_{Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\Pi((\Delta_{X})_{\sharp}\mu_{X},(\Delta_{Y})_{\sharp}\mu_{Y})\\
\rho\in\Pi(\mu_{X},\mu_{Y})\end{subarray}}\int\int\big|d_{X}(h(x_{0}),x_{1})-d_{Y}(h^{\prime}(y_{0}),y_{1})\big|\,\gamma(dx_{0}{\times}dx_{1}{\times}dy_{0}{\times}dy_{1})\rho(dh{\times}dh^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\Pi((\Delta_{X})_{\sharp}\mu_{X},(\Delta_{Y})_{\sharp}\mu_{Y})\\
\rho\in\Pi(\mu_{X},\mu_{Y})\end{subarray}}\int\int\big|d_{X}(x_{2},x_{1})-d_{Y}(y_{2},y_{1})\big|\,\gamma(dx_{0}{\times}dx_{1}{\times}dy_{0}{\times}dy_{1})\rho(dx_{2}{\times}dy_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\Pi((\Delta_{X})_{\sharp}\mu_{X},(\Delta_{Y})_{\sharp}\mu_{Y})\\
\rho\in\Pi(\mu_{X},\mu_{Y})\end{subarray}}\int\int\big|d_{X}(x_{2},x_{1})-d_{Y}(y_{2},y_{1})\big|\,(\pi_{2,4})_{\sharp}\gamma(dx_{1}{\times}dy_{1})\rho(dx_{2}{\times}dy_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\Pi(\mu_{X},\mu_{Y})\\
\rho\in\Pi(\mu_{X},\mu_{Y})\end{subarray}}\int\int\big|d_{X}(x_{2},x_{1})-d_{Y}(y_{2},y_{1})\big|\,\gamma(dx_{1}{\times}dy_{1})\rho(dx_{2}{\times}dy_{2}).

as desired.
∎

Theorem
81
.

First, for any
h\in H
,
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
, we can bound the Wasserstein distance between their loss profiles by

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{W},1}((\ell_{h})_{\sharp}\eta,(\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}})_{\sharp}\eta^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\tau\in\Pi((\ell_{h})_{\sharp}\eta,(\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}})_{\sharp}\eta^{\prime})}\int|a-b|\,\tau(da{\times}db)

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}\int|a-b|\,(\ell_{h}\times\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}})_{\sharp}(da{\times}db)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}\int|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})|\,\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime}).

Similarly, we can bound

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{W},p}^{d_{\mathrm{W},1}}({\mathcal{L}}(P),{\mathcal{L}}(P^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\inf_{\tau\in\Pi((F_{P})\sharp\lambda,(F_{P})\sharp\lambda^{\prime})}\left(\int d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{p}(\mu,\nu)\,\tau(d\mu{\times}d\nu)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\rho\in\Pi(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})}\left(\int d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{p}(\mu,\nu)\,(F_{P}\times F_{P^{\prime}})_{\sharp}\rho(d\mu{\times}d\nu)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\rho\in\Pi(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})}\left(\int d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{p}((\ell_{h})_{\sharp}\eta,(\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}})_{\sharp}\eta^{\prime})\,\rho(dh{\times}dh^{\prime})\right)^{1/p}.

Combining these bounds gives

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{W},p}^{d_{\mathrm{W},1}}({\mathcal{L}}(P),{\mathcal{L}}(P^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\rho\in\Pi(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})}\left(\int\left(\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}\int|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})|\,\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})\right)^{p}\,\rho(dh{\times}dh^{\prime})\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\rho,\gamma}\left(\int\left(\int|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})|\,\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})\right)^{p}\,\rho(dh{\times}dh^{\prime})\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle=d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P^{\prime}),

as desired.
∎

Theorem
82
.

The support of a probability measure on a Polish space is
\sigma
-compact, so without loss of generality, we can assume that
Y
is
\sigma
-compact by replacing
Y
with the support of the
Y
-marginal of
\eta
. Write
Y
as an increasing sequence of compact subsets
(Y_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
. For each
n
, define
\pi_{n}:Y\to Y_{n}\cup\{\bullet\}
by

\displaystyle\pi_{n}(y):=\begin{cases}y&y\in Y_{n}\\
\bullet&\text{else}\end{cases}.

We then define a sequence of weighted problems
(P_{n},\lambda_{n})
. Define

P_{n}:=(X,Y_{n}\cup\{\bullet\},\eta_{n},\ell_{n},H_{n})

where

•

\eta_{n}:=(\id_{X}\times\pi_{n})_{\sharp}\eta

•

\ell_{n}(y,y^{\prime}):=1_{Y_{n}\times Y_{n}}(y,y^{\prime})\ell(y,y^{\prime})

•

H_{n}:=\left\{\pi_{n}\circ h\middle|h\in H\right\}
.

Furthermore, if we let
\pi_{n*}:H\to H_{n}
be given by
\pi_{n*}(h):=\pi_{n}\circ h
, we can define
\lambda_{n}:=(\pi_{n*})_{\sharp}\lambda
.

We claim that
d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P_{n})
converges to 0. Since
\eta_{n}=(\id_{X}\times\pi_{n})_{\sharp}\eta
, the function
\id_{X}\times\pi_{n}
induces a coupling between
\eta
and
\eta_{n}
. Similarly, since
\lambda_{n}=(\pi_{n*})_{\sharp}\lambda
, the function
\pi_{n*}
induces a coupling between
\lambda
and
\lambda_{n}
. By definition of
d_{\mathrm{R},p}
, choosing these couplings produces a bound on
d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P_{n})
.

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R},p}(P,P_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int\left(\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell^{\prime}(\pi\circ h(x),\pi(y))\big|\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)\right)^{p}\lambda(dh)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle=\left(\int\left(\int 1_{(Y_{n}\times Y_{n})^{c}}(h(x),y)\,\ell(h(x),y)\,\eta(dx\times dy)\right)^{p}\lambda(dh)\right)^{1/p}.

Here the superscript
c
represents set complement.
Notice that the indicator
1_{(Y_{n}{\times}Y_{n})^{c}}(h(x),y)
converges pointwise to 0 as
n\to\infty
.
We apply the dominated convergence theorem twice to finish the proof. Indeed, by assumption,

\displaystyle\diam_{p}(P)=\left(\int\left(\int\ell(h(x),y)\,\eta(dx\times dy)\right)^{p}\lambda(dh)\right)^{1/p}<\infty.

(7)

In particular, (
7
) tells us that
\ell(h(x),y)
is integrable as a function of
(x,y)
for
\lambda
-a.e.
h\in H
. Since
\ell(h(x),y)
dominates
1_{(Y_{n}\times Y_{n})^{c}}(h(x),y)\ell(h(x),y)
, the dominated convergence theorem tells us that

\displaystyle\int 1_{(Y_{n}\times Y_{n})^{c}}(h(x),y)\ell(h(x),y)\,\eta(dx\times dy)\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0

for
\lambda
-a.e.
h\in H
.
Additionally, (
7
) tells us that

\displaystyle\left(\int\ell(h(x),y)\,\eta(dx\times dy)\right)^{p}

is an integrable function of
h
. Using the dominated convergence theorem again,

\displaystyle\left(\int\left(\int 1_{(Y_{n}\times Y_{n})^{c}}(h(x),y)\ell(h(x),y)\,\eta(dx\times dy)\right)^{p}\lambda(dh)\right)^{1/p}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0

as desired.
∎

Proposition
83
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Let
Q
be the partition of
Y
provided by Theorem
65
, and let

P_{Q}=(X,Q,\eta_{Q},\ell_{Q},H_{Q})

be the coarsened problem. Let
R_{\pi}\in{\mathcal{C}}(H,H_{Q})
be the correspondence induced by the surjection
\pi_{*}(h)=\pi\circ h
, and let
\gamma_{(\id_{X}\times\pi)}\in\Pi(\eta,\eta_{Q})
be the coupling induced by the map
\id_{X}\times\pi
. Recall the proof of Theorem
65
, in which we demonstrated that
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P_{Q})<\varepsilon
by showing

\dis_{P,P_{Q}}(R_{\pi},\gamma_{(\id_{X}\times\pi)})<\varepsilon.

Define a measure
\lambda_{Q}=(\pi_{*})_{\sharp}\lambda
on
H_{Q}
and let
\rho_{\pi}\in\Pi(\lambda,\lambda_{Q})
be the coupling induced by
\pi_{*}
. Then, since
R_{\pi}
is the support of
\rho_{\pi}
,

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{R},p}((P,\lambda),(P_{Q},\lambda_{Q}))\leq\dis_{P,P_{Q},p}(\rho_{\pi},\gamma_{\id_{X}\times\pi})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\int\left(\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell_{Q}(h^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\big|\,\gamma_{\id_{X}\times\pi}(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})\right)^{p}\rho_{\pi}(dh{\times}dh^{\prime})\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R_{\pi}}\int\big|\ell(h(x),y)-\ell_{Q}(h^{\prime}(x^{\prime}),y^{\prime})\big|\,\gamma_{\id_{X}\times\pi}(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dis_{P,P_{Q}}(R_{\pi},\gamma_{\id_{X}\times\pi})<\varepsilon,

as desired.
∎

Theorem
91
.

Proposition
89
provides the bound
d_{\mathrm{R}}(P,P_{N})\leq d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{s_{\ell,\lambda,p}}(\eta,\eta\cdot N)
.
One can check that if
\tau
is a coupling of the Markov kernels
\delta_{\id_{X\times Y}}
and
N
, then
\eta\cdot\tau\in\Pi(\eta,\eta\cdot N)
. Therefore we can write

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{s_{\ell,\lambda,p}}(\eta,\eta\cdot N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta\cdot N)}\int_{(X\times Y)^{2}}s_{\ell,\lambda,p}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))\,\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\inf_{\tau\in\Pi(\delta_{\id_{X\times Y}},N)}\int_{(X\times Y)^{2}}s_{\ell,\lambda,p}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))\,(\eta\cdot\tau)(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{\tau\in\Pi(\delta_{\id_{X\times Y}},N)}\int_{X\times Y}\int_{(X\times Y)^{2}}s_{\ell,\lambda,p}((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2}))\,\tau(x,y)(dx_{1}{\times}dy_{1}{\times}dx_{2}{\times}dy_{2})\,\eta(dx{\times}dy)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{W},1}^{s_{\ell,\lambda,p}}(\delta_{\id_{X\times Y}},N),

which is the desired bound.
∎

Lemma
95
.

Let

R:=\left\{(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})\in H_{1}{\times}H_{2}{\times}H_{3}\middle|(h_{1},h_{2})\in R_{1,2},(h_{2},h_{3})\in R_{2,3}\right\}.

We have the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{1,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{2,3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}

Every pictured map is a projection map. We need only check that they are inverse connected, meaning that the preimage of any connected set is connected. Let
A\subseteq H_{1}
be a connected set. Then
p_{1}^{-1}(A)
is connected, and so is
B:=p_{2}(p_{1}^{-1}(A))
. Similarly,
C:=q_{2}(q_{1}^{-1}(B))
is connected. Noting finally that the preimage of
A
in
R
is

r_{1}^{-1}(p_{1}^{-1})(A)=A\times B\times C,

we see that it is a product of connected sets and hence connected. The proof for preimages of connected sets in
H_{2}
and
H_{3}
is similar.
∎

Theorem
100
.

For the first inequality, suppose
\widehat{f},\widehat{g}:Z\to{\mathbb{R}}
are continuous functions inducing the Reeb graphs
\reeb(P)
and
\reeb(P^{\prime})
respectively. Then

\|\widehat{f}-\widehat{g}\|_{\infty}\geq\left|\inf_{z\in Z}\widehat{f}(z)-\inf_{z\in Z}\widehat{g}(z)\right|=\left|\inf_{h\in H}{\mathcal{R}}_{P}(h)-\inf_{h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}}{\mathcal{R}}_{P^{\prime}}(h^{\prime})\right|=|{\mathcal{B}}(P)-{\mathcal{B}}(P^{\prime})|

where the equality comes from the fact that the infimum of a function can be determined from the Reeb graph that it induces.

Now suppose
d_{\operatorname{RC}}(P,P^{\prime})<a
. Then we can pick a correspondence
R\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{con}}(H,H^{\prime})
that witnesses this fact, giving us the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{P^{\prime}}}

Recall
{\mathcal{R}}_{P}
is the risk function associated with problem
P
. We can then induce two Reeb graphs from functions on
R
: one induced by
{\mathcal{R}}_{P}\circ p_{1}
and the other induced by
{\mathcal{R}}_{P^{\prime}}\circ p_{2}
. The former Reeb graph is isomorphic to
\reeb(P)
since
p_{1}
is a continuous surjection with connected fibers, and similarly the latter is isomorphic to
\reeb(P^{\prime})
. Hence

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{U}}(\reeb(P),\reeb(P^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}|{\mathcal{R}}_{P}\circ p_{1}(h,h^{\prime})-{\mathcal{R}}_{P}\circ p_{1}(h,h^{\prime})|

\displaystyle=\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}|{\mathcal{R}}_{P}(h)-{\mathcal{R}}_{P}(h^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\eta^{\prime})}\sup_{(h,h^{\prime})\in R}\int\big|\ell_{h}(x,y)-\ell_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big|\,\gamma(dx{\times}dy{\times}dx^{\prime}{\times}dy^{\prime})<a.

Hence
d_{\mathrm{U}}(\reeb(P),\reeb(P^{\prime}))<a
as well.
∎

Appendix B
Weak Isomorphism with Classification Problems

While the infimum in the definition of the Risk distance makes it difficult to characterize when two problems are distance zero apart in general, the closely related notion of weak isomorphism is simpler to deal with. For instance, we can characterize which problems are weakly isomorphic to classification problems.

Definition 104
.

Call a problem
P
rectangular if there exists a finite partition
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m}
of the response space
Y
and, for any
h\in H
, a finite partition
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
of the input space
X
such that

\ell_{h}(x,y)=\sum_{ij}a_{ij}1_{X_{i}^{h}}(x)1_{Y_{j}}(y)\quad\eta{\text{-a.e.}}.

A problem
P
is rectangular, for instance, if the loss is a sum of indicators on rectangles. That is, if

\ell(y_{1},y_{2})=\sum_{ij}a_{ij}1_{Y_{i}}(y_{1})1_{Y_{j}}(y_{2})

for some partition
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}\subseteq Y
, then
P
is rectangular.

Theorem 105
.

A problem is weakly isomorphic to a classification problem if and only if it is rectangular.

The proof makes use of the notion of a
generated partition
. Let
S
be a set and let
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}
be a collection of subsets of
S
. Then the
partition of
S
generated by
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}
is the partition of
S
induced by the equivalence relation
{\sim}
where
s\sim s^{\prime}
means that
s\in T_{i}\iff s^{\prime}\in T_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
Note that since there are finitely many
T_{i}
, the generated partition has finitely many blocks as well.

Proof.

Suppose
P
is rectangular and let
Q:=\{Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m}\}
,
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
, and
(a_{ij})_{ij}
be as in the definition of a rectangular problem, and let
\pi_{Q}:Y\to Q
be the quotient map. If
m\neq n
, pad the partitions appropriately with copies of the empty set and pad the matrix
(a_{ij})_{ij}
with zeros to make
m=n
. Define a classification problem

P^{\prime}:=(X,Q,(\id_{X}\times\pi_{Q})_{\sharp}\eta,\ell^{\prime},H^{\prime})

where

•

\ell^{\prime}(Y_{i},Y_{j}):=a_{ij}

•

H^{\prime}=\left\{\phi(h)\middle|h\in H\right\}
, where
\phi(h):X\to Q
sends
x
to
Y_{i}
if
x\in X^{h}_{i}
.

We claim
P^{\prime}
is a simulation of
P
via the maps
\id_{X}
and
\pi_{Q}
. Indeed, for any
h\in H
, we can calculate that
\ell^{\prime}_{\phi(h)}(x,\pi_{Q}(y))=a_{ij}
if
x\in X^{h}_{i}
and
y\in Y_{j}
. Hence
\ell^{\prime}_{\phi(h)}(x,\pi_{Q}(y))=\ell_{h}(x,y)
for any
h\in H
, showing that
P^{\prime}
is a simulation of
P
. In particular, the two are weakly isomorphic.

For the right hand implication, we proceed in two parts. First suppose that
P
is a simulation of a classification problem
P^{\prime}
, whose response space is
Y^{\prime}=\{y_{1}^{\prime},\dots,y_{m}^{\prime}\}
. Let the simulation be via the maps
f_{1}:X\to X^{\prime}
and
f_{2}:Y\to Y^{\prime}
. We will show that
P
is rectangular. By the definition of simulation, for any
h\in H
, we can select an
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
such that
\ell_{h}(x,y)=\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(f_{1}(x),f_{2}(y))

\eta
-almost everywhere. Since the function
\ell^{\prime}
is on a finite set
Y^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}
, we can write it with a finite sum of indicators to get that,
\eta
-almost everywhere,

\displaystyle\ell_{h}(x,y)=\ell^{\prime}(h^{\prime}\circ f_{1}(x),f_{2}(y))

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}\ell^{\prime}(y_{i}^{\prime},y_{j}^{\prime})1_{Y_{i}}(h^{\prime}\circ f_{1}(x))1_{Y_{j}}(f_{2}(y))

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}\ell^{\prime}(y_{i}^{\prime},y_{j}^{\prime})1_{h^{\prime}\circ f_{1}^{-1}(Y_{i})}(x)1_{f_{2}^{-1}(Y_{j})}(y).

Hence
P
is rectangular.

Next we prove that if
P
is rectangular and simulates
P^{\prime}
, then
P^{\prime}
must be rectangular as well. Let the simulation again be via maps
f_{1}:X\to X^{\prime}
and
f_{2}:Y\to Y^{\prime}
, and let
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
,
\{Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
and
(a_{ij})_{ij}
be as in the definition of a rectangular problem. Consider the family

{\mathcal{Y}}:=\left\{f_{2}[Y_{i}]\middle|1\leq i\leq m\right\}

of subsets of
Y^{\prime}
. The sets in
{\mathcal{Y}}
do not necessarily form a partition of
Y^{\prime}
, so consider the partition
Q_{Y^{\prime}}
generated by
{\mathcal{Y}}
. Now let
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
be arbitrary and, using the definition of a simulation, pick
h\in H
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}\times f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}}

commutes.
Consider the family

{\mathcal{X}}^{h^{\prime}}:=\left\{f_{1}[X^{h}_{i}]\middle|1\leq i\leq m\right\}.

Again, these sets do not necessarily partition
X^{\prime}
, so let
Q_{X^{\prime}}^{h^{\prime}}
be the partition of
X^{\prime}
generated by
{\mathcal{X}}^{h^{\prime}}
.

We claim that, if
A\in Q_{X^{\prime}}^{h^{\prime}}
and
B\in Q_{Y^{\prime}}
, then
\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}
is constant
\eta^{\prime}
-almost everywhere on
A\times B
. Indeed, by construction of
Q_{X^{\prime}}^{h^{\prime}}
and
Q_{Y^{\prime}}
, we have that
f_{1}[X^{h}_{i}]\supseteq A
and
f_{2}[Y_{j}]\supseteq B
for some
i
and
j
. Since
\ell_{h}
is constant on
X^{h}_{i}\times Y_{j}

\eta
-almost everywhere, by commutativity of the above diagram we also have that
\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}
is constant
\eta^{\prime}
-almost everywhere on
(f_{1}\times f_{2})[X^{h}_{i}\times Y_{j}]\supseteq A\times B
. Therefore,
\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}
can be written as a linear combination of indicators on such rectangles:

\ell^{\prime}_{h^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\sum_{ij}a_{ij}1_{A_{i}}(x)1_{B_{j}}(y)

for some
(a_{ij})_{ij}
. Here we have numbered the blocks in the partitions
Q_{X^{\prime}}^{h^{\prime}}=\{A_{1},\dots,A_{m}\}
and
Q_{Y^{\prime}}=\{B_{1},\dots,B_{n}\}
. Hence
P^{\prime}
is rectangular.

Now, if
P
is weakly isomorphic to a classification problem
P^{\prime}
via a common simulation
P^{\prime\prime}
, we have shown that
P^{\prime\prime}
, as a simulation of a classification problem
P^{\prime}
, must be rectangular. Furthermore, since
P^{\prime\prime}
is rectangular and simulates
P
, we have shown that
P
must be rectangular as well.
∎
